) of a generator function
f

in the problem of the strict monotonicity of the metrological bound (
102
) can be understood
by considering the dominance arrows in the
s_{2}
vs.
f
plot.
For dominance-monotone
f
, these run always upwards and to the right/left if
f
is increasing/decreasing
but never horizontally,
since
s_{2}
is a strictly increasing dominance-monotone (
75
).
The refinement arrows do the same, since refinement implies dominance (
133
)
but dominance-monotonicity gives more constraints.

Now, the failure of strict monotonicity of the bound (
102
) occurs when
there exist partitions
\hat{\xi},\hat{\xi}^{\prime}\in\hat{P}_{\text{I}}

of
f(\hat{\xi})=:k\lessgtr f(\hat{\xi}^{\prime})=:k^{\prime}
,
which maximize
s_{2}
among those partitions
(
s_{2}(\hat{\xi})=\max_{\hat{\upsilon}:f(\hat{\upsilon})=k}s_{2}(\hat{\upsilon})
,

s_{2}(\hat{\xi}^{\prime})=\max_{\hat{\upsilon}:f(\hat{\upsilon}^{\prime})=k^{\prime}}s_{2}(\hat{\upsilon}^{\prime})
),
and

b_{f}(k)=s_{2}(\hat{\xi})\geq s_{2}(\hat{\xi}^{\prime})
, and the bound does not increase,
b_{f}(k)=b_{f}(k^{\prime})
see (
76a
).
This, however, is excluded if
f
is dominance-monotone
and

\hat{\xi}
and
\hat{\xi}^{\prime}
are related by dominance,
since if
\hat{\xi}
is dominated by
\hat{\xi}^{\prime}

then
s_{2}(\hat{\xi})<s_{2}(\hat{\xi}^{\prime})
and
f(\hat{\xi})\lessgtr f(\hat{\xi}^{\prime})
hold together,
and the bound does increase,
b_{f}(k)<b_{f}(k^{\prime})
.
If, on the contrary,
\hat{\xi}
and
\hat{\xi}^{\prime}
are related by dominance

but

f
is not dominance-monotone,
then it might happen that
\hat{\xi}
dominates
\hat{\xi}^{\prime}

(while still
f(\hat{\xi})=k\lessgtr f(\hat{\xi}^{\prime})=k^{\prime}
is the case)
then
b_{f}(k)=s_{2}(\hat{\xi})>s_{2}(\hat{\xi}^{\prime})
, and the bound does not increase,
b_{f}(k)=b_{f}(k^{\prime})
,
which can be illustrated by case of toughness (see Figure
8
).
For
n=6
, we have
\hat{\xi}:=\{5,1\}
, dominating
\hat{\xi}^{\prime}:=\{4,2\}
,
so
s_{2}(\hat{\xi})=26>s_{2}(\hat{\xi}^{\prime})=20
, while
t(\hat{\xi})=1<t(\hat{\xi}^{\prime})=2
,
and these partitions are also of maximal squareability in the inverse images of
t
,
so
b_{\text{tgh}}(1)=b_{\text{tgh}}(2)=26
.
If
\hat{\xi}
and
\hat{\xi}^{\prime}
are not related by dominance,
then imposing dominance-monotonicity of
f
does not exclude the above possibility
of the failure of the strict monotonicity (
102
) of the bound.
That is, dominance-monotonicity is not sufficient for the bound to be strictly monotone (
102
)
for all
k\in f(\hat{P}_{\text{I}})
.
This can be illustrated by the case of
s_{3}
(
59a
) (see Figure
9
).
Although
s_{3}
is dominance-monotone (
138d
),
but the bound
b_{s_{3}}(k)
is not strictly monotone:
for
n=6
, we have
\hat{\xi}:=\{2,2,2\}
and
\hat{\xi}^{\prime}:=\{3,1,1,1\}
,
not related by dominance,
and
s_{3}(\hat{\xi})=24<s_{3}(\hat{\xi}^{\prime})=30
,
but
s_{2}(\hat{\xi})=s_{2}(\hat{\xi}^{\prime})=12
, and these partitions are also of maximal squareability in the inverse images of
s_{3}
,
so
b_{s_{3}}(24)=b_{s_{3}}(30)=12
.

One may object that dominance-monotonicity may not be necessary either for the bound to be strictly monotone (
102
)
for all
k\in f(\hat{P}_{\text{I}})
.
This is because the strict monotonicity of the bound (
102
)
is determined by the maximal values of
s_{2}
in the inverse images
f^{-1}(k)
,
while for other partitions
\hat{\xi}\in f^{-1}(k)
for which
s_{2}(\hat{\xi})<b_{f}(k)
,
the dominance-monotonicity could fail,
while not affecting the
b_{f}(k)
values of the bounds.

Anyway, the main point is that
the overall number of possibilities of the failure of the (
102
) strict monotonicity of the bound
is greatly reduced by the imposing of dominance-monotonicity.
This is because the dominance lattice is significantly more ‘dense’ than the refinement poset,
there are seemingly few pairs of partitions not related by dominance
and

which, in the same time, could break the (
102
) strict monotonicity of the bound.
On the other hand,
the significance of dominance-monotonicity is that
it is the definitive property of generalized entropies,
expressing the mixedness of the subsystem size distribution
(see the discussions in Sections
4.4
and
4.7
, Appendix
E.1
,
and further in Section
5.11
).

5.11
Remarks

Here we list some remarks
on the connections of one-parameter partial entanglement properties and quantum metrology,
one paragraph each.

Note that
convex roof entanglement measures,
and, in fact, any proper entanglement measures for mixed states,
are infeasible to evaluate in general,
this is why bounds on those are so important.
In our case,
these bounds can not only be measured experimentally,
but also calculated by (
70a
).
On the other hand, these are convex roof extensions of depths (
89b
),
the meaning of which is easy to understand, especially in the particular cases of
squareability (
90
) and producibility (
92
),
see also the explanations in Section
5.6
.

Note that
the partial separability is a dimension-independent classification,
while the metrological bounds are given only for
d=2
, qubits or spin-
1/2
systems,
used directly in experiments.
The generalization for larger local Hilbert space dimension
d=\dim(\mathcal{H}_{l})
is, however, straigthforward.
Let us have the collective operator
A=\sum_{l=1}^{n}A_{l}\otimes\mathrm{I}_{\overline{\{l\}}}
,
where the minimal and maximal eigenvalues of
A_{l}
are
a_{\text{min}}
and
a_{\text{max}}
uniformly for all
l
.
The only proof affected is of (
72
),
where only (
142c
) has to be changed to (
142a
)
(see Appendix
F
),
which leads to the

\max_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{\hat{\xi}}}\frac{F_{\text{Q}}(\pi,A)}{(a_{\text{max}}-a_{\text{min}})^{2}}=s_{2}(\hat{\xi})

(106)

generalization of (
72
).
Then every bound in Section
5
can be given for/by
the normalized quantum Fisher information
F_{\text{Q}}(\pi,A)/(a_{\text{max}}-a_{\text{min}})^{2}

on the left-hand side.
Such bounds are independent of the dimension
d
of the local Hilbert spaces,
and depend only on the uniform ‘spectral width’
a_{\text{max}}-a_{\text{min}}
of the local operators.
The
J^{\text{z}}
measurement of spin-
s
systems can be of particular interest,
then
d=2s+1
, and
(a_{\text{max}}-a_{\text{min}})^{2}=4s^{2}
.

Note that entanglement can also be detected efficiently
by
spin squeezing inequalities

[
100
,
101
,
102
,
103
,
104
,
105
,
106
]
, and
even the entanglement depth can be detected

[
1
,
8
,
9
,
10
,
11
,
107
,
3
,
4
,
5
,
14
,
15
]
.
A promising research direction is then to give spin squeezing bounds on other one-parameter multipartite entanglement properties.
For example,
for macroscopic singlet states
[
102
]
,
also demonstrated experimentally in both cold and hot atomic systems
[
105
,
106
]
,
we have the property
\hat{\xi}=\{2,2,\dots,2\}
;
and also the
s_{-\infty}
number of spins unentangled with the rest (
127d
)
can be bounded from above with the measurement results.

6
Summary

We began this study with elaborating the general theory of
one-parameter families of partial entanglement properties (Section
2
),
and the resulting entanglement depth like quantities (Section
3
).
The main idea came by observing that the partial entanglement property
leading to entanglement depth is producibility,
which is given by sub-level sets of the width of the Young diagram of the partition of the system (
14b
).
One-parameter partial entanglement properties, called
f
-entanglement
,
could then be defined by sub- or super-level sets of
generator functions

f
(
27
),
for which the only requirement necessary for the construction was the
monotonicity
(
22
).
The resulting one-parameter classification of quantum states is
the chain hierarchy (
31b
) of
nested state spaces
(
30a
) of
(k,f)
-entangled states
,
and the ‘layers’ of
disjoint classes
(
34
) of
strictly
(k,f)
-entangled states
.
We also had the

f
-entanglement depth
and
f
-entanglement depth of formation
,
being the generalization (
38
) and (
49
)
of entanglement depth (
37b
) and entanglement depth of formation (
48b
)
with respect to these one-parameter partial entanglement properties,
for which we showed LOCC monotonicity.

We also provided an ample supply of one-parameter entanglement properties (Section
4
)
by the corresponding generator functions,
beyond the
entanglement partitionability
,
producibility
and
stretchability
,
the most notable ones were
the
entanglement squareability
,
defined by the sum of squares of the sizes of entangled subsystems (
64
),
the
entanglement toughness
,
defined by the size of the smallest entangled subsystem (
58
),
the
entanglement degree of freedom
,
defined by the effective number of elementary subsystems needed for the description of the state (
68
),
and the
Rényi property of entanglement
,
defined by the Rényi function of the subsystem size distribution (
62b
),
nicely interpolating among producibility, partitionability and toughness.

The formulation of metrological multipartite entanglement criteria (Section
5
)
fits well into the framework of one-parameter entanglement properties.
We showed that for states of any given partial separability property,
the upper bound for the quantum Fisher information in collective spin-z measurement of qubits
is given by the squareability of that property (
73
), (
76a
).
This could be evaluated for
f
-entanglement in some cases,
and is given by a function of the
f
-entanglement depth,
which turns out to be the depth of an induced one-parameter property (
79
).
This led to an identity if that property itself is the squareability (
86
),
suggesting that entanglement squareability is the natural multipartite entanglement property
from the point of view of quantum metrology.
Since the quantum Fisher information is the convex roof extension of the variance (
71b
),
thanks to the similar formulations of the
f
-entanglement depth (
43
)
and the
f
-entanglement depth of formation (
49
),
these bounds could be strengthened,
and the quantum Fisher information turned out to bound
the average of the depth (depth of formation (
49
))
of the corresponding induced one-parameter property (
89b
)
from below.
In particular,
F_{\text{Q}}/n
puts a lower bound on the average size of the entangled subsystems for random choice of elementary subsystems,
which is much smaller than the size of the largest entangled subsystem in any decomposition.

While deriving the metrological bounds with respect to different one-parameter partial entanglement properties,
in the case of toughness (
85
)
we were faced with the possibility that
a given one-parameter property may not lead to different bounds for different values of the parameter
k
.
This arises when the bound (
76a
) fails to be strictly monotone (
102
),
in which case the one-parameter property cannot be detected for those particular values of
k
.
It turned out that the two extreme cases are those of producibility (or partitionability, etc.), where the bound is strictly monotone for all
k
,
and of toughness, where the bound is nowhere strictly monotone, apart from one
k
value.
The possible failure of the strict monotonicity (
102
) of the bound
comes from the interplay (
76a
) of the generator function of the given one-parameter property with squareability.
Imposing the dominance-monotonicity of the one-parameter property
significantly reduces the possibilities of the failure of strict monotonicity (
102
) of the bound,
which singles out one-parameter properties given by entropies (dominance-monotone functions) of the subsystem size distributions.

Appendix A
Usual measures of
f
-entanglement

In this section we recall the

entanglement of formation
and
relative entropy of entanglement

of the permutation invariant partial entanglement properties
[
20
]
,
and we also define those with respect to the one-parameter entanglement properties.

A.1
Measures of partial entanglement properties

There are several possible ways for quantifying entanglement
(LOCC monotones, entanglement monotones
[
74
,
75
,
76
,
77
,
78
]
),
which, in some cases, can also be applied for the particular kinds of multipartite entanglement
[
18
,
20
]
.
Here we recall the
entanglement of formation

[
75
,
84
]

and the
relative entropy of entanglement

[
108
,
109
,
110
]
,
which fit well to the hierarchy of properties (
11b
),
in the sense that they
give measures for each permutation invariant multipartite entanglement property
\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\hat{P}_{\text{II}}
,
and these measures are related in the same way as the properties (multipartite monotones
[
18
,
20
]
).

On the first level,
for all integer partitions
\hat{\xi}\in\hat{P}_{\text{I}}
, we have the

\hat{\xi}
-entanglement of formation
, being the average
\hat{\xi}
-entanglement of the optimal decomposition
[
20
,
18
]
,
and the

relative entropy of
\hat{\xi}
-entanglement
, being the distinguishability from the
\hat{\xi}
-separable states,

\displaystyle E^{\text{oF}}_{\hat{\xi}}(\rho)

\displaystyle:=\min_{\{(p_{j},\pi_{j})\}\vdash\rho}\sum_{j}p_{j}\min_{\xi\in s^{-1}(\hat{\xi})}\sum_{X\in\xi}S_{X}(\pi_{j}),

(107)

\displaystyle E^{\text{R}}_{\hat{\xi}}(\rho)

\displaystyle:=\min_{\sigma\in\mathcal{D}_{\hat{\xi}}}S(\rho||\sigma),

(108)

where

S_{X}(\rho)=S(\rho_{X})=-\Tr(\rho_{X}\ln(\rho_{X}))

is the
von Neumann entropy

[
111
,
112
,
113
,
114
]

of the state
\rho_{X}=\Tr_{\overline{X}}(\rho)

of subsystem
X
(where
\overline{X}=\{1,2,\dots,n\}\setminus X
),
and
S(\rho||\sigma)=\Tr(\rho\ln(\rho)-\rho\ln(\sigma))

is the
Umegaki relative entropy
or
quantum Kullback-Leibler divergence

[
115
,
112
,
113
,
114
]
.
(The first minimization in (
107
) is taken over all the
\rho=\sum_{i}p_{i}\pi_{i}
pure convex decompositions of
\rho
,
for which we use the shorthand notation
\vdash
above.
This is the so called
convex roof extension

[
83
,
84
]

of the
\hat{\xi}
-correlation
[
20
,
18
]
.
The second minimization in (
107
) is taken over all the set partitions
\xi=\{X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{{\lvert\xi\rvert}}\}

which have parts of sizes
\hat{\xi}=\{x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}}\}
.
For more details, see
[
20
]
.)
These are entanglement monotones
[
18
,
20
,
108
]

(convex and nonincreasing on average with respect to selective LOCC
[
76
,
77
,
78
]
,
for the relative entropy of
\hat{\xi}
-entanglement this follows from the general construction
[
108
]
),
faithful
(
E^{\text{oF/R}}_{\hat{\xi}}(\rho)=0
if and only if
\rho\in\mathcal{D}_{\hat{\xi}}
),
and multipartite monotone
[
20
,
18
]
, which is

\hat{\upsilon}\preceq\hat{\xi}\;\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\;E^{\text{oF/R}}_{\hat{\upsilon}}\geq E^{\text{oF/R}}_{\hat{\xi}},

(109)

expressing that any state is more entangled with respect to a finer partition.

On the second level,
for all down-sets of integer partitions
\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\hat{P}_{\text{II}}
we have the

\boldsymbol{\hat{\xi}}
-entanglement of formation
, being the average
\hat{\xi}
-entanglement of the optimal decomposition
[
20
,
18
]
, and the

relative entropy of
\boldsymbol{\hat{\xi}}
-entanglement
, being the distinguishability from the
\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
-separable states,

\displaystyle E^{\text{oF}}_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\rho)

\displaystyle:=\min_{\{(p_{j},\pi_{j})\}\vdash\rho}\sum_{j}p_{j}\min_{\xi\in\vee s^{-1}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}})}\sum_{X\in\xi}S_{X}(\pi_{j}),

(110)

\displaystyle E^{\text{R}}_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\rho)

\displaystyle:=\min_{\sigma\in\mathcal{D}_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}}S(\rho||\sigma).

(111)

(The second minimization in (
110
) is taken over all the set partitions
\xi=\{X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{{\lvert\xi\rvert}}\}

which have parts of sizes
\hat{\xi}\in\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
.
For more details, see
[
20
]
.)
These are entanglement monotones
[
18
,
20
,
108
]
,
faithful
(
E^{\text{oF/R}}_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}(\rho)=0
if and only if
\rho\in\mathcal{D}_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
),
and multipartite monotone
[
20
,
18
]
, which is

\hat{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}\preceq\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\;\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\;E^{\text{oF/R}}_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\upsilon}}}\geq E^{\text{oF/R}}_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}},

(112)

expressing that any state is more entangled with respect to a finer multipartite entanglement property.

A.2
Examples of
f
-entanglement measures

For the three notable one-parameter families of properties,
partitionability, producibility and stretchability (
14
),
we have by (
110
)
the
k
-partitionability entanglement of formation
,
the
k
-producibility entanglement of formation
,
and the
k
-stretchability entanglement of formation

[
20
,
18
]
,
as well as by (
111
)
the
relative entropy of
k
-partitionability of entanglement
,
the
relative entropy of
k
-producibility of entanglement
,
and the
relative entropy of
k
-stretchability of entanglement
,
for the respective ranges of
k
,

\displaystyle E^{\text{oF/R}}_{k\text{-part}}(\rho)

\displaystyle:=E^{\text{oF/R}}_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k\text{-part}}}(\rho),

(113a)

\displaystyle E^{\text{oF/R}}_{k\text{-prod}}(\rho)

\displaystyle:=E^{\text{oF/R}}_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k\text{-prod}}}(\rho),

(113b)

\displaystyle E^{\text{oF/R}}_{k\text{-str}}(\rho)

\displaystyle:=E^{\text{oF/R}}_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k\text{-str}}}(\rho),

(113c)

with the monotonicity

\displaystyle k\geq k^{\prime}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\;E^{\text{oF/R}}_{k\text{-part}}\geq E^{\text{oF/R}}_{k^{\prime}\text{-part}},

(114a)

\displaystyle k\leq k^{\prime}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\;E^{\text{oF/R}}_{k\text{-prod}}\geq E^{\text{oF/R}}_{k^{\prime}\text{-prod}},

(114b)

\displaystyle k\leq k^{\prime}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\;E^{\text{oF/R}}_{k\text{-str}}\geq E^{\text{oF/R}}_{k^{\prime}\text{-str}}.

(114c)

by (
15
) and (
112
).

A.3
f
-entanglement measures in general

In general,
for the one-parameter properties (
27
),
defined by the generator function
f
over the permutation invariant properties (
22
),
we have the
(k,f)
-entanglement of formation

and the
relative entropy of
(k,f)
-entanglement

E^{\text{oF/R}}_{k,f}(\rho):=E^{\text{oF/R}}_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k,f}}(\rho)

(115)

by (
110
) and (
111
).
These are entanglement monotones,
faithful (
E^{\text{oF/R}}_{k,f}(\rho)=0
if and only if
\rho\in\mathcal{D}_{k,f}
),
by the corresponding properties of
E^{\text{oF/R}}_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
,
and monotone in
k

k\lesseqgtr k^{\prime}\;\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\;E^{\text{oF/R}}_{k,f}\geq E^{\text{oF/R}}_{k^{\prime},f},

(116)

by (
28
) and (
112
).

Note that
if the generator functions
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are both increasing,
then for all
k_{2}\in f_{2}(\hat{P}_{\text{I}})

f_{1}\leq f_{2}\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;E^{\text{oF/R}}_{k_{2},f_{2}}\geq E^{\text{oF/R}}_{k_{1}(k_{2}),f_{1}}

(117)

for
k_{1}(k_{2}):=\min\{k\in f_{1}(\hat{P}_{\text{I}})\;|\;k_{2}\leq k\}
;
while if
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
are both decreasing,
then for all
k_{2}\in f_{2}(\hat{P}_{\text{I}})

f_{1}\leq f_{2}\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;E^{\text{oF/R}}_{k_{1},f_{1}}\geq E^{\text{oF/R}}_{k_{2}(k_{1}),f_{2}}

(118)

for
k_{2}(k_{1}):=\max\{k\in f_{2}(\hat{P}_{\text{I}})\;|\;k_{1}\geq k\}
.
(Indeed,

\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k_{2},f_{2}}\preceq\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k_{1}(k_{2}),f_{1}}
and

\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k_{1},f_{1}}\preceq\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k_{2}(k_{1}),f_{2}}
are easy to check in the two cases
by definition (
27
),
then (
112
) with the definition (
115
) leads to the claim.)

Note that, if a generator function
f:\hat{P}_{\text{I}}\to\mathbb{R}

is composed with a
monotone
function
g:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
,
the measures (
115
) may decrease with respect to this,

E^{\text{oF/R}}_{g(k),g\circ f}\leq E^{\text{oF/R}}_{k,f},

(119a)

while if
g
is
strictly
monotone,
then the measures (
115
) are invariant with respect to this,

E^{\text{oF/R}}_{g(k),g\circ f}=E^{\text{oF/R}}_{k,f},

(119b)

by the result (
121
), the monotonicity (
112
) and the definition (
115
).

For partitionability, producibility and stretchability,
we get back the measures (
113
)-(
114
)
by the height, width and rank (
23
) as generator functions.

Appendix B
f
-entanglement depth

In this section we show the validity of the
formulations (
41
), (
43
) and (
49
)
of
f
-entanglement depth and
f
-entanglement depth of formation
in Section
3
.

B.1
f
-entanglement depth by membership

Here we prove the formula (
41
)
for the case of increasing
f
,

\min_{k\in f(\hat{P}_{\text{I}})}\bigl\{k\;\big|\;\rho\in\mathcal{D}_{k,f}\bigr\}=\min_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\hat{P}_{\text{II}}}\bigl\{f(\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}})\;\big|\;\rho\in\mathcal{D}_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\bigr\},

see (
38
) and (
41
),
then similar reasoning works for the case of decreasing
f
.

First,

if
\min\{k^{\prime}\;|\;\rho\in\mathcal{D}_{k^{\prime},f}\}=k

then
\rho\in\mathcal{D}_{k,f}\equiv\mathcal{D}_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k,f}}
by (
30a
),
therefore

\min_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\hat{P}_{\text{II}}}\bigl\{f(\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}})\;\big|\;\rho\in\mathcal{D}_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\bigr\}\leq k\equiv\min_{k^{\prime}\in f(\hat{P}_{\text{I}})}\bigl\{k^{\prime}\;\big|\;\rho\in\mathcal{D}_{k^{\prime},f}\bigr\}

by (
29
).

Second,

if
\min_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\{f(\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}})\;|\;\rho\in\mathcal{D}_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\}=k

then there exists
\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\hat{P}_{\text{II}}

for which
f(\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}})\equiv\max_{\hat{\xi}\in\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}f(\hat{\xi})=k
such that
\rho\in\mathcal{D}_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}
,
then
\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\preceq\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k,f}
by (
25a
) and (
27
),
then
\rho\in\mathcal{D}_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k,f}}\equiv\mathcal{D}_{k,f}
by (
11b
) and (
30a
),
therefore

\min_{k^{\prime}\in f(\hat{P}_{\text{I}})}\bigl\{k^{\prime}\;\big|\;\rho\in\mathcal{D}_{k^{\prime},f}\bigr\}\leq k\equiv\min_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}\in\hat{P}_{\text{II}}}\bigl\{f(\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}})\;\big|\;\rho\in\mathcal{D}_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}}\bigr\}

by (
31b
).

B.2
f
-entanglement depth by decomposition

Here we prove the formula (
43
)
for the case of increasing
f
,

\min_{k\in f(\hat{P}_{\text{I}})}\bigl\{k\;\big|\;\rho\in\mathcal{D}_{k,f}\bigr\}=\min_{\{(p_{i},\pi_{i})\}\vdash\rho}\max\limits_{i}\bigl\{D_{f}(\pi_{i})\bigr\},

see (
38
) and (
43
),
then similar reasoning works for the case of decreasing
f
.
(Note that the minimum with respect to the decompositions is attained
for a given finite length of decompositions,
since
D_{f}
takes finite number of discrete values
f(\hat{P}_{\text{I}})
,
and so is
\max_{i}D_{f}(\pi_{i})
.)

First,

if
\min\{k^{\prime}\;|\;\rho\in\mathcal{D}_{k^{\prime},f}\}=k

then
\rho\in\mathcal{D}_{k,f}=\Conv(\mathcal{P}_{k,f})
by (
30a
),
that is, there exists a pure decomposition
\{(p_{i},\pi_{i})\}
of
\rho
,
such that
\pi_{i}\in\mathcal{P}_{k,f}
for all
i
,
that is,
D_{f}(\pi_{i})\leq k
for all
i
by (
30c
) and (
38
),
then
\max_{i}D_{f}(\pi_{i})\leq k
for that decomposition,
therefore

\min_{\{(p_{i},\pi_{i})\}\vdash\rho}\max_{i}D_{f}(\pi_{i})\leq k\equiv\min_{k^{\prime}\in f(\hat{P}_{\text{I}})}\bigl\{k^{\prime}\;\big|\;\rho\in\mathcal{D}_{k^{\prime},f}\bigr\}.

Second,

if
\min_{\{(p_{i},\pi_{i})\}\vdash\rho}\max_{i}D_{f}(\pi_{i})=k
,
then there exists a pure decomposition
\{(p_{i},\pi_{i})\}
of
\rho

for which
D_{f}(\pi_{i})\leq\max_{i}D_{f}(\pi_{i})=k
for all
i
,
then
\pi_{i}\in\mathcal{P}_{D_{f}(\pi_{i}),f}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{k,f}
for all
i
by (
39b
) and (
31a
),
then
\rho=\sum_{i}p_{i}\pi_{i}\in\Conv(\mathcal{P}_{k,f})\equiv\mathcal{D}_{k,f}
by (
30a
),
therefore

\min\bigl\{k^{\prime}\;\big|\;\rho\in\mathcal{D}_{k^{\prime},f}\bigr\}\leq k\equiv\min_{\{(p_{i},\pi_{i})\}\vdash\rho}\max_{i}D_{f}(\pi_{i})

by (
31b
).

B.3
f
-entanglement depth of formation

Here
first we recall and slightly extend some results on convex roof construction
[
83
,
84
]
,
then prove the validity of the formula (
49
).

In general, for a function
g:\mathcal{P}\to\mathbb{R}
,
the functions
g^{\cup/\cap}:\mathcal{D}\to\mathbb{R}
given as

\displaystyle g^{\cup}(\rho):=\inf_{\{(p_{i},\pi_{i})\}\vdash\rho}\sum_{i}p_{i}g(\pi_{i}),

(120a)

\displaystyle g^{\cap}(\rho):=\sup_{\{(p_{i},\pi_{i})\}\vdash\rho}\sum_{i}p_{i}g(\pi_{i}),

(120b)

are the (unique)
largest convex
and
smallest concave extensions
of
g
to
\mathcal{D}

[
83
,
84
]
.
These are clearly extensions,
g^{\cup}(\pi)=g^{\cap}(\pi)=g(\pi)
for all pure states
\pi\in\mathcal{P}
.
To see that
g^{\cup/\cap}
are indeed the largest convex/smallest concave extensions,
we have that for all convex/concave extensions
G:\mathcal{D}\to\mathbb{R}

for all pure convex decompositions
\rho=\sum_{i}p_{i}\pi_{i}
of all
\rho\in\mathcal{D}
,

G(\rho)\lesseqgtr\sum_{i}p_{i}G(\pi_{i})=\sum_{i}p_{i}g(\pi_{i}),

then
G(\rho)\lesseqgtr g^{\cup/\cap}(\rho)
.
To see that
g^{\cup/\cap}
are indeed convex/concave,
we have that
for all pure convex decompositions

\rho_{j}=\sum_{i}p_{j,i}\pi_{j,i}

of all
\rho_{j}\in\mathcal{D}
,
such that
for all
\epsilon>0
,

0\leq\sum_{i}p_{j,i}g(\pi_{j,i})-g^{\cup}(\rho_{j})<\epsilon
,
or

0\leq g^{\cap}(\rho_{j})-\sum_{i}p_{j,i}g(\pi_{j,i})<\epsilon
,
in the two cases, respectively,
and
for weights
0\leq w_{j}
,
\sum_{j}w_{j}=1
,

\begin{split}&g^{\cup/\cap}\Bigl(\sum_{j}w_{j}\rho_{j}\Bigr)\lesseqgtr\sum_{j}\sum_{i}w_{j}p_{j,i}g^{\cup/\cap}(\pi_{j,i})\\
&\quad=\sum_{j}w_{j}\sum_{i}p_{j,i}g(\pi_{j,i})\\
&\quad\lesseqgtr\sum_{j}w_{j}g^{\cup/\cap}(\rho_{j})+\sum_{j}w_{j}\epsilon,\end{split}

which holds for arbitrarily small
\epsilon
.

If
g:\mathcal{P}\to\mathbb{R}
is lower/upper semicontinuous,
then the infimum in the definition of
g^{\cup}
, respectively the supremum in the definition of
g^{\cap}
is attained.
(Then these are convex/concave
roof
extensions.
The proof of the continuous case
[
83
,
84
]
is generalized here.)
To see this,
let us consider the graph of the function
g

in
\Lin_{\text{SA}}(\mathcal{H})\oplus\mathbb{R}
,

\mathcal{G}(g):=\bigl\{(\pi,g(\pi))\;\big|\;\pi\in\mathcal{P}\bigr\},

and for
y\in\mathbb{R}
its restriction (sub/super-level graph)

\mathcal{G}_{y}(g):=\bigl\{(\pi,g(\pi))\;\big|\;\pi\in\mathcal{P},g(\pi)\lesseqgtr y\bigr\}\subset\mathcal{G}(g).

It is known that a function
g:\mathcal{P}\to\mathbb{R}
is lower/upper semicontinuous
if and only if its sub/super-level sets

\mathcal{P}_{y}(g):=\bigl\{\pi\in\mathcal{P}\;\big|\;g(\pi)\lesseqgtr y\bigr\}

are closed for all
y\in\mathbb{R}
.
Then
\mathcal{G}_{y}(g)
is closed, and also compact, since
\mathcal{P}
is compact.
Then the convex hull

\Conv\bigl(\mathcal{G}_{y}\bigr)\subset\Lin_{\text{SA}}(\mathcal{H})\oplus\mathbb{R}

is also compact.
On the other hand, its extremal points are
\Extr\bigl(\Conv(\mathcal{G}_{y})\bigr)=\mathcal{G}_{y}
.
(Indeed,
the points
(\pi,g(\pi))\in\mathcal{G}_{y}
are extremal, since
the elements of
\Conv(\mathcal{G}_{y})
are of the form

\sum_{i}p_{i}(\pi_{i},g(\pi_{i}))=\bigl(\sum_{i}p_{i}\pi_{i},\sum_{i}p_{i}g(\pi_{i})\bigr)
with
\pi_{i}\in\mathcal{P}_{y}(g)\subseteq\mathcal{P}
,
which equals to a
(\pi,g(\pi))
with
\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{y}(g)\subseteq\mathcal{P}
if and only if the mixture is trivial.
So we have
\Extr\bigl(\Conv(\mathcal{G}_{y})\bigr)\supseteq\mathcal{G}_{y}
,
and the other inclusion
\Extr\bigl(\Conv(\mathcal{G}_{y})\bigr)\subseteq\mathcal{G}_{y}
is obvious by definition.)
For a
\rho\in\Conv\bigl(\mathcal{P}_{y}(g)\bigr)
,
we form the intersection

\{(\rho,y^{\prime})\;|\;y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}\}\cap\Conv\bigl(\mathcal{G}_{y}(g)\bigr)
,
which is compact (since
\Conv\bigl(\mathcal{G}_{y}(g)\bigr)
is compact), so it is a line segment
\{(\rho,y^{\prime})\;|\;y^{\prime}\in[y_{\text{min}},y_{\text{max}}]\}
.
Since this segment is in
\Conv\bigl(\mathcal{G}_{y}(g)\bigr)
,
all of its points can be written as
(\rho,y^{\prime})=\sum_{i}p_{i}(\pi_{i},g(\pi_{i}))=\bigl(\rho,\sum_{i}p_{i}g(\pi_{i})\bigr)

for
\pi_{i}\in\mathcal{P}_{y}(g)
,
so
g^{\cup}(\rho)=y_{\text{min}}=\sum_{i}p_{i}g(\pi_{i})
in the lower semicontinuous case
and
g^{\cap}(\rho)=y_{\text{max}}=\sum_{i}p_{i}g(\pi_{i})
in the upper semicontinuous case.
Note that
\dim\bigl(\Conv\bigl(\mathcal{G}_{y}(g)\bigr)\bigr)=\dim(\mathcal{D})+1=(d^{2n}-1)+1=d^{2n}
,
and the points
(\rho,y_{\text{min}})
or
(\rho,y_{\text{max}})
belong to its boundary, being a face of dimension at most
d^{2n}-1
,
so, due to Carathéodory’s theorem, the length of the decomposition above is at most
(d^{2n}-1)+1=d^{2n}
.

Now,
to see the validity of the formula (
49
),
we need that

D_{f}|_{\mathcal{P}}:\mathcal{P}\to\mathbb{R}
are
lower/upper semicontinuous for increasing/decreasing generator functions
f
.
A function is lower/upper semicontinuous
if and only if its sub/super-level sets are closed.
In our case,
let us have for
y\in\mathbb{R}

k(y)=\begin{cases}\max\{k^{\prime}\in f(\hat{P}_{\text{I}})\;|\;k^{\prime}\leq y\},\\
\min\{k^{\prime}\in f(\hat{P}_{\text{I}})\;|\;k^{\prime}\geq y\},\end{cases}

for increasing and decreasing
f
, respectively,
then the sub/super-level sets of
D_{f}|_{\mathcal{P}}
for increasing/decreasing
f
are just

\mathcal{P}_{y}(D_{f}|_{\mathcal{P}})=\bigl\{\pi\in\mathcal{P}\;\big|\;D_{f}(\pi)\lesseqgtr y\bigr\}=\mathcal{D}_{k(y),f}\cap\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}_{k(y),f}

see (
40b
),
which are closed (
30c
).

Appendix C
Coarsenings and symmetries

In this section we consider the natural transformations of
the one-parameter entanglement properties, given in Section
2
,
and of the resulting depths, given in Section
3
.

C.1
Coarsenings and symmetries of
f
-entanglement

If a generator function
f:\hat{P}_{\text{I}}\to\mathbb{R}

is composed with a
monotone
function
g:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
,
then the composition
g\circ f
is also a generator function,
and it leads to the (possibly coarser) properties.

\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{g(k),g\circ f}\succeq\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k,f},

(121a)

while if
g
is
strictly
monotone, then
the resulting generator function
g\circ f
,
although takes different values,
leads to the same properties (same sub- or super-level sets (
27
)),

\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{g(k),g\circ f}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k,f},

(121b)

as the original generator function
f
.

To see (
121a
),
for all
k^{\prime}\in g(f(\hat{P}_{\text{I}}))
,
for all
k\in f(\hat{P}_{\text{I}})
such that
g(k)=k^{\prime}
,
we have

\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k^{\prime},g\circ f}\succeq\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k,f}.

(122a)

Indeed, by the definition (
27
),
for all
\hat{\xi}\in\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k,f}

we have
f(\hat{\xi})\lesseqgtr k
,
then
g(f(\hat{\xi}))\lesseqgtr g(k)
for increasing
g

or
g(f(\hat{\xi}))\gtreqless g(k)
for decreasing
g
,
both lead to
\hat{\xi}\in\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{g(k),g\circ f}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k^{\prime},g\circ f}
(
27
).

To see (
121b
),
we have the inverse function
g^{-1}

in the case of strictly monotone
g
,
so using (
121a
) with
g
and also with
g^{-1}

we have

\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k,f}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{g^{-1}(g(k)),g^{-1}\circ g\circ f}\succeq\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{g(k),g\circ f}\succeq\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k,f}
,
then the antisymmetry of the partial order
\preceq
leads to the claim.

Note that the main point here is that the effect of the transformation
g

is just a possible coarsening of the classification.
For all
k^{\prime}\in g(f(\hat{P}_{\text{I}}))
,
we have

\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k^{\prime},g\circ f}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k,f}\;\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\;k=\begin{cases}\max\bigl(g^{-1}(\{k^{\prime}\})\bigr),\\
\min\bigl(g^{-1}(\{k^{\prime}\})\bigr),\end{cases}

(122b)

for increasing or decreasing
f
, respectively.
(Here
g^{-1}(\{k^{\prime}\})=\{k\in f(\hat{P}_{\text{I}})\;|\;g(k)=k^{\prime}\}
is the inverse image of
g
,
since
g
is not necessarily strictly monotone, that is, not invertible in general.)
Indeed,
to see the
‘if’ direction
,

\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k^{\prime},g\circ f}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k,f}\;\;\Longleftarrow\;\;k=\begin{cases}\max\bigl(g^{-1}(\{k^{\prime}\})\bigr),\\
\min\bigl(g^{-1}(\{k^{\prime}\})\bigr),\end{cases}

we have the
\succeq
relation in (
122a
),
and we need to see the
\preceq
relation,
that is,
for all
k^{\prime}\in g(f(\hat{P}_{\text{I}}))
, for all
\hat{\xi}\in\hat{P}_{\text{I}}
,
assuming increasing
g
,

g(f(\hat{\xi}))\lesseqgtr k^{\prime}\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;f(\hat{\xi})\lesseqgtr\begin{cases}\max\bigl(g^{-1}(\{k^{\prime}\})\bigr),\\
\min\bigl(g^{-1}(\{k^{\prime}\})\bigr),\end{cases}

by (
27
),
which is equivalent by contraposition to that

g(f(\hat{\xi}))\gtrless k^{\prime}\;\;\Longleftarrow\;\;f(\hat{\xi})\gtrless\begin{cases}\max\bigl(g^{-1}(\{k^{\prime}\})\bigr),\\
\min\bigl(g^{-1}(\{k^{\prime}\})\bigr),\end{cases}

which holds, because from the right-hand side

g(f(\hat{\xi}))\gtreqless k^{\prime}
follows by the increasing monotonicity of
g
,
and also
f(\hat{\xi})\notin g^{-1}(\{k^{\prime}\})
which is equivalent to
g(f(\hat{\xi}))\neq k^{\prime}

(this is where we used that not any element of the inverse image
g^{-1}(\{k^{\prime}\})
is used, but only the maximal/minimal one).
The proof of this point goes analogously for decreasing
g
.
To see the
‘only if’ direction
,
we use the contraposition

\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k^{\prime},g\circ f}\neq\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k^{\prime\prime},f}\;\;\Longleftarrow\;\;k^{\prime\prime}\neq\begin{cases}\max\bigl(g^{-1}(\{k^{\prime}\})\bigr),\\
\min\bigl(g^{-1}(\{k^{\prime}\})\bigr),\end{cases}

which can be seen
by already having that
\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k^{\prime},g\circ f}=\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k,f}

for
k=\max/\min(g^{-1}(\{k^{\prime}\}))
,
so it cannot be equal to
\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k^{\prime\prime},f}
for another
k^{\prime\prime}\neq\max/\min(g^{-1}(\{k^{\prime}\}))
,
since
for all
k,k^{\prime\prime}\in f(\hat{P}_{\text{I}})
we have

\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k,f}\neq\hat{\boldsymbol{\xi}}_{k^{\prime\prime},f}
if and only if
k\neq k^{\prime\prime}

by the order isomorphism (
28
) and standard order theory.

Having the two chains (
28
) of one-parameter entanglement properties,
the meaning of (
122
) is that
(k^{\prime},g\circ f)
-entanglement properties are

(k,f)
-entanglement properties for specific
k
values (which are maximal or minimal among those which are mapped to
k^{\prime}
by
g
),
the
(k,f)
-entanglement properties for the other
k
values do not appear among the
(k^{\prime},g\circ f)
-entanglement properties.

C.2
Coarsenings and symmetries of
f
-entanglement state spaces and classes

If a generator function
f:\hat{P}_{\text{I}}\to\mathbb{R}

is composed with a
monotone
function
g:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
,
then the composition
g\circ f

leads to the (possibly larger) state spaces (
30
) and (possibly coarser) classes (
34
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{g(k),g\circ f}

\displaystyle\supseteq\mathcal{D}_{k,f},

(123a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{k^{\prime},g\circ f}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{k\in g^{-1}(\{k^{\prime}\})}\mathcal{C}_{k,f}

(123b)

for all
k^{\prime}\in g(f(\hat{P}_{\text{I}}))
,
while if
g
is
strictly
monotone, then
the resulting generator function
g\circ f
,
although taking different values,
leads to the same state spaces (
30
) and classes (
34
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{g(k),g\circ f}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}_{k,f},

(123c)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{g(k),g\circ f}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{C}_{k,f},

(123d)

as the original generator function
f
.

We have (
123a
) by
(
121a
), (
11b
) and the definition (
30a
).

To see (
123b
),
let us denote

g^{-1}(\{k^{\prime}\})=\{k_{1},k_{2},\dots k_{m}\}
, where
k_{i}\gtrless k_{j}
if
i<j
,

k_{1}=\max/\min\bigl(g^{-1}(\{k^{\prime}\})\bigr)

and also the next value
k_{m+1}=\max/\min\bigl(g^{-1}(\{k^{\prime}_{\mp}\})\bigr)
,
then we have

\mathcal{C}_{k^{\prime},g\circ f}=\mathcal{D}_{k^{\prime},g\circ f}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{k^{\prime}_{\mp},g\circ f}=\mathcal{D}_{k_{1},f}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{k_{m+1},f}

by definition (
34
) and the result (
122b
),
then we have
\mathcal{D}_{k_{i},f}\supseteq\mathcal{D}_{k_{j},f}
if
i<j
by (
31b
),
so the above is

\begin{split}=(\mathcal{D}_{k_{1},f}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{k_{2},f})&\cup(\mathcal{D}_{k_{2},f}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{k_{3},f})\cup\dots\\
&\qquad\qquad\dots\cup(\mathcal{D}_{k_{m-1},f}\setminus\mathcal{D}_{k_{m},f}),\end{split}

which can be seen by the consecutive application of the set identity

(A_{1}\setminus A_{2})\cup(A_{2}\setminus A_{3})=A_{1}\setminus A_{3}

for the nested sets
A_{1}\supseteq A_{2}\supseteq A_{3}
.

We have (
123c
) by
(
121b
), (
11b
) and the definition (
30a
).

We have (
123d
) by
(
123c
) and the definition (
34
).

C.3
Coarsenings and symmetries of
f
-entanglement depth

Here we prove the formula in (
44
).
For all
k^{\prime}\in g(f(\hat{P}_{\text{I}}))

we have
D_{g\circ f}(\rho)=k^{\prime}
if and only if
\rho\in\mathcal{C}_{k^{\prime},g\circ f}
by (
39a
),
then
\rho\in\mathcal{C}_{k,f}
for a
k\in f(\hat{P}_{\text{I}})
for which
g(k)=k^{\prime}
by (
123b
),
which holds if and only if
D_{f}(\rho)=k
by (
39a
) again,
then
g(D_{f}(\rho))=g(k)=k^{\prime}=D_{g\circ f}(\rho)
.

C.4
Coarsenings and symmetries of
f
-entanglement depth of formation

Here we prove the formulas in (
53
).

To see the case (
53a
) of convex
g
,
for increasing generator function
f:\hat{P}_{\text{I}}\to\mathbb{R}
(
22
)
and increasing convex function
g:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
,
we have

\begin{split}&D^{\text{oF}}_{g\circ f}\overset{\eqref{eq:DepthOFf}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\min\limits_{\{(p_{j},\pi_{j})\}\vdash\rho}\sum_{j}p_{j}D_{g\circ f}(\pi_{j})\\
&\quad\overset{\eqref{eq:gmeasuresfD}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\min\limits_{\{(p_{j},\pi_{j})\}\vdash\rho}\sum_{j}p_{j}g\bigl(D_{f}(\pi_{j})\bigr)\\
&\quad{\;\;\geq\;\;}\min\limits_{\{(p_{j},\pi_{j})\}\vdash\rho}g\Bigl(\sum_{j}p_{j}D_{f}(\pi_{j})\Bigr)\\
&\quad{\;\;=\;\;}g\Bigl(\min\limits_{\{(p_{j},\pi_{j})\}\vdash\rho}\sum_{j}p_{j}D_{f}(\pi_{j})\Bigr)\overset{\eqref{eq:DepthOFf}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}g\bigl(D^{\text{oF}}_{f}\bigr),\end{split}

where
the
first equality
holds by definition (
49
), because
g\circ f
is increasing if both
f
and
g
are increasing;
the
second equality
holds because (
44
) holds for all monotone
g
;
the
inequality
holds by the convexity of
g
, and because a pointwise smaller function has smaller minimum;
the
third equality
holds because
g
is increasing;
and
the
last equality
holds by definition (
49
), because
f
is increasing.
For decreasing
f
and
g
, we have increasing
g\circ f
,
and the derivation above holds with slight changes:
after the
third equality

\min
is changed to
\max
, because
g
is decreasing;
and
but the
last equality
still holds by definition (
49
), because
f
is now decreasing.
For decreasing
f
and increasing
g
, we have decreasing
g\circ f
,
and the derivation above holds with slight changes:
after the
first equality

\min
is changed to
\max
, because
g\circ f
is decreasing;
and
the
last equality
still holds by definition (
49
), because
f
is now decreasing.
For increasing
f
and decreasing
g
, we have decreasing
g\circ f
,
and the derivation above holds with slight changes:
after the
first equality

\min
is changed to
\max
, because
g\circ f
is decreasing;
but after the
third equality

\max
is changed back to
\min
, because
g
is decreasing;
and
the
last equality
holds again by definition (
49
), because
f
is increasing.

The case (
53b
) for concave
g
can be seen similarly,
only the inequality is flipped in the derivation above.

The case (
53c
) for affine
g
follows from (
53a
) and (
53b
),
by noting that an affine function is both convex
and
concave.

Appendix D
General properties of power-based generator functions

In this section we show some properties
of power-based functions in Section
4

together with proof for the convenience of the reader.

D.1
Some basic tools for power functions

Here we recall some basic facts about power functions,
with emphasis on the precise ranges.

For
0<x\in\mathbb{R}
,
q,q^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

1\lesseqgtr x:\quad q\leq q^{\prime}\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;x^{q}\lesseqgtr x^{q^{\prime}}.

(124)

(This can easily be seen by taking the logarithm, or by the derivative
\partial x^{q}/\partial q=x^{q}\ln(x)
.)
For
0<x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
,
q\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

0\lesseqgtr q:\quad x\leq x^{\prime}\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;x^{q}\lesseqgtr(x^{\prime})^{q}.

(125)

(This can easily be seen by taking the logarithm, or by the derivative
\partial x^{q}/\partial x=qx^{q-1}
.)
For
0<x_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
,
q\in\mathbb{R}
, we also have for the finite sums

1\lesseqgtr q:\quad\sum_{i=1}^{m}x_{i}^{q}\lesseqgtr\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{m}x_{i}\Bigr)^{q}.

(126)

(This is simply because

\frac{\sum_{i}x_{i}^{q}}{\bigl(\sum_{j}x_{j}\bigr)^{q}}=\sum_{i}\bigl(\frac{x_{i}}{\sum_{j}x_{j}}\bigr)^{q}\lesseqgtr\sum_{i}\bigl(\frac{x_{i}}{\sum_{j}x_{j}}\bigr)^{1}=1
,
where
0<\frac{x_{i}}{\sum_{j}x_{j}}\leq 1
, so the inequalities in the two cases
1\lesseqgtr q

arise by applying the lower relation in (
124
).)

D.2
q
-limits of power based generator functions

Here we list the limits of the generator functions
studied in Section
4

with respect to the parameter
q
.
Although many of these are more or less well known,
we collect these together with the proofs for the convenience of the reader.

\displaystyle s_{0}(\hat{\xi})

\displaystyle=h(\hat{\xi})\equiv{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert},

(127a)

\displaystyle s_{1}(\hat{\xi})

\displaystyle=n,

(127b)

\displaystyle\lim_{q\to+\infty}s_{q}(\hat{\xi})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}n&\text{if $\hat{\xi}=\bot$},\\
\infty&\text{else},\end{cases}

(127c)

\displaystyle s_{-\infty}(\hat{\xi})

\displaystyle:=\lim_{q\to-\infty}s_{q}(\hat{\xi})={\big\lvert\{x\in\hat{\xi}\;|\;x=1\}\big\rvert}

(127d)

\displaystyle\lim_{q\to 0^{-}}N_{q}(\hat{\xi})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}n&\text{if $\hat{\xi}=\top$},\\
0&\text{else},\end{cases}

(127e)

\displaystyle\lim_{q\to 0^{+}}N_{q}(\hat{\xi})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}n&\text{if $\hat{\xi}=\top$},\\
\infty&\text{else},\end{cases}

(127f)

\displaystyle N_{1}(\hat{\xi})

\displaystyle=n,

(127g)

\displaystyle N_{+\infty}(\hat{\xi})

\displaystyle:=\lim_{q\to+\infty}N_{q}(\hat{\xi})=\max(\hat{\xi}),

(127h)

\displaystyle N_{-\infty}(\hat{\xi})

\displaystyle:=\lim_{q\to-\infty}N_{q}(\hat{\xi})=\min(\hat{\xi}),

(127i)

\displaystyle M_{0}(\hat{\xi})

\displaystyle:=\lim_{q\to 0}M_{q}(\hat{\xi})=\Bigl(\prod\hat{\xi}\Bigr)^{1/{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}},

(127j)

\displaystyle M_{1}(\hat{\xi})

\displaystyle:=n/{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert},

(127k)

\displaystyle M_{+\infty}(\hat{\xi})

\displaystyle:=\lim_{q\to+\infty}M_{q}(\hat{\xi})=\max(\hat{\xi}),

(127l)

\displaystyle M_{-\infty}(\hat{\xi})

\displaystyle:=\lim_{q\to-\infty}M_{q}(\hat{\xi})=\min(\hat{\xi}),

(127m)

\displaystyle T_{0}(\hat{\xi})

\displaystyle={\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}-1,

(127n)

\displaystyle T_{1}(\hat{\xi})

\displaystyle:=\lim_{q\to 1}T_{q}(\hat{\xi})=S(\hat{\xi}),

(127o)

\displaystyle T_{+\infty}(\hat{\xi})

\displaystyle:=\lim_{q\to+\infty}T_{q}(\hat{\xi})=0,

(127p)

\displaystyle\lim_{q\to-\infty}T_{q}(\hat{\xi})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}0&\text{if $\hat{\xi}=\top$},\\
\infty&\text{else},\end{cases}

(127q)

\displaystyle R_{0}(\hat{\xi})

\displaystyle=\ln({\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}),

(127r)

\displaystyle R_{1}(\hat{\xi})

\displaystyle:=\lim_{q\to 1}R_{q}(\hat{\xi})=S(\hat{\xi}),

(127s)

\displaystyle R_{+\infty}(\hat{\xi})

\displaystyle:=\lim_{q\to+\infty}R_{q}(\hat{\xi})=\ln(n)-\ln(\max(\hat{\xi})),

(127t)

\displaystyle R_{-\infty}(\hat{\xi})

\displaystyle:=\lim_{q\to-\infty}R_{q}(\hat{\xi})=\ln(n)-\ln(\min(\hat{\xi})),

(127u)

\displaystyle P_{0}(\hat{\xi})

\displaystyle={\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert},

(127v)

\displaystyle P_{1}(\hat{\xi})

\displaystyle:=\lim_{q\to 1}P_{q}(\hat{\xi})=\mathrm{e}^{S(\hat{\xi})},

(127w)

\displaystyle P_{+\infty}(\hat{\xi})

\displaystyle:=\lim_{q\to+\infty}P_{q}(\hat{\xi})=n/\max(\hat{\xi}),

(127x)

\displaystyle P_{-\infty}(\hat{\xi})

\displaystyle:=\lim_{q\to-\infty}P_{q}(\hat{\xi})=n/\min(\hat{\xi}).

(127y)

We have (
127a
) and (
127b
) by definition (
59a
).

To see (
127c
) for (
59a
),
we have that

\lim_{q\to\infty}x^{q}=1
, or
\infty
if
x=1
or
1<x
, respectively,
and the subsystem sizes
x\in\hat{\xi}
are
1\leq x
,
and all of those are
1
if and only if
\hat{\xi}=\bot
.

To see (
127d
) for (
59a
),
we have that

\lim_{q\to-\infty}x^{q}=1
, or
0
if
x=1
or
1<x
, respectively.

To see (
127e
) and (
127f
) for (
59b
),
if
\hat{\xi}=\top
then we have
N_{q}(\top)=n
without respect to
q
.
If
\hat{\xi}\neq\top
then we have

\lim_{q}N_{q}(\hat{\xi})=\lim_{q}\mathrm{e}^{\ln(N_{q}(\hat{\xi}))}=\mathrm{e}^{\lim_{q}\ln(N_{q}(\hat{\xi}))}
,
for which

\lim_{q\to 0^{\pm}}\ln(N_{q}(\hat{\xi}))=\lim_{q\to 0^{\pm}}\frac{1}{q}\ln\bigl(\sum_{x\in\hat{\xi}}x^{q}\bigr)=\bigl(\lim_{q\to 0^{\pm}}\frac{1}{q}\bigr)\bigl(\lim_{q\to 0^{\pm}}\ln(\sum_{x\in\hat{\xi}}x^{q})\bigr)=\bigl(\lim_{q\to 0^{\pm}}\frac{1}{q}\bigr)\ln({\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert})=\pm\infty

where
\ln({\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert})>0
since
{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}>1
since
\hat{\xi}\neq\top
,
leading to the claim.

We have (
127g
) by definition (
59b
).

To see (
127h
) for (
59b
),
we have
x\leq x_{\text{max}}:=\max(\hat{\xi})
,
so, for
q\geq 0
,
we have
x^{q}\leq x_{\text{max}}^{q}
by (
125
),
which leads to the second inequality in

0\leq q:\quad x_{\text{max}}^{q}\leq\sum_{x\in\hat{\xi}}x^{q}\leq{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}x_{\text{max}}^{q}.

(128a)

Applying the
q
-th root for
q>0
to this
gives

x_{\text{max}}\leq N_{q}(\hat{\xi})\leq{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}^{1/q}x_{\text{max}}
by (
125
).
Taking the limit
q\to+\infty
leads to the claim.

To see (
127i
) for (
59b
),
we have
x\geq x_{\text{min}}:=\min(\hat{\xi})
,
so, for
q\leq 0
,
we have
x^{q}\leq x_{\text{min}}^{q}
by (
125
),
which leads to the second inequality in

0\geq q:\quad x_{\text{min}}^{q}\leq\sum_{x\in\hat{\xi}}x^{q}\leq{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}x_{\text{min}}^{q}.

(128b)

Applying the
q
-th root for
q<0
to this gives

x_{\text{min}}\geq N_{q}(\hat{\xi})\geq{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}^{1/q}x_{\text{min}}
by (
125
).
Taking the limit
q\to-\infty
leads to the claim.

To see (
127j
) for (
59c
),
we have

\lim_{q}M_{q}(\hat{\xi})=\lim_{q}\mathrm{e}^{\ln M_{q}(\hat{\xi})}=\mathrm{e}^{\lim_{q}\ln M_{q}(\hat{\xi})}
,
for which we have

\lim_{q\to 0}\ln(M_{q}(\hat{\xi}))=\lim_{q\to 0}\frac{1}{q}\ln\bigl(\frac{1}{{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}}\sum_{x\in\hat{\xi}}x^{q}\bigr)
,
which, using L’Hôpital rule for the
0/0
-type limit, is

\lim_{q\to 0}\frac{\sum_{x\in\hat{\xi}}x^{q}\ln(x)}{\sum_{x\in\hat{\xi}}x^{q}}=\frac{1}{{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}}\sum_{x\in\hat{\xi}}\ln(x)=\ln((\prod_{x\in\hat{\xi}}x)^{1/{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}})
,
leading to the claim.

We have (
127k
) by definition (
59c
).

To see (
127l
) for (
59c
),
we recall (
128a
).
Multiplying this with
{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}^{-1}
and
applying the
q
-th root for

to this
gives

{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}^{-1/q}x_{\text{max}}\leq M_{q}(\hat{\xi})\leq x_{\text{max}}
by (

).
Taking the limit
q\to+\infty
leads to the claim.

To see (
127m
) for (
59c
),
we recall (
128b
).
Multiplying this with
{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}^{-1}
and
applying the
q
-th root for

to this
gives

{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}^{-1/q}x_{\text{min}}\geq M_{q}(\hat{\xi})\geq x_{\text{min}}
by (
125
).
Taking the limit
q\to-\infty
leads to the claim.

We have (
127n
) by definition (
62a
).

To see (
127o
) for (
62a
),
we use L’Hôpital rule for the
0/0
-type limit,

\lim_{q\to 1}\frac{1}{1-q}(\sum_{x\in\hat{\xi}}(x/n)^{q}-1)=\lim_{q\to 1}-\sum_{x\in\hat{\xi}}(x/n)^{q}\ln(x/n)=-\sum_{x\in\hat{\xi}}(x/n)\ln(x/n)
,
which is (
62c
).

To see (
127p
) for (
62a
),
we have that
x/n\leq 1
,
so the nominator is bounded, while the denominator grows to
-\infty
,
leading to the claim.

To see (

) for (
62a
),
we have that if
\hat{\xi}\neq\top
then
x/n<1
,
so the nominator grows exponentially, while the denominator grows only linearly,
leading to the claim.
If
\hat{\xi}=\top=\{n\}
then there is only one summand,

x/n=1
, leading to the claim.

We have (
127r
) by definition (
62b
).

To see (
127s
) for (
62b
),
we use L’Hôpital rule for the
0/0
-type limit,

\lim_{q\to 1}\frac{1}{1-q}\ln(\sum_{x\in\hat{\xi}}(x/n)^{q})=\lim_{q\to 1}\frac{-\sum_{x\in\hat{\xi}}(x/n)^{q}\ln(x/n)}{\sum_{x\in\hat{\xi}}(x/n)^{q}}=-\sum_{x\in\hat{\xi}}(x/n)\ln(x/n)
,
which is (
62c
).

To see (
127t
) for (
62b
),
we recall (
128a
).
Multiplying this with
1/n^{q}
, applying
\ln()
and multiplying with
1/(1-q)

for
