gives

\frac{q}{1-q}\ln(x_{\text{max}}/n)\geq R_{q}(\hat{\xi})\geq\frac{\ln({\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert})}{1-q}+\frac{q}{1-q}\ln(x_{\text{max}}/n)
.
Taking the limit
q\to+\infty
leads to the claim.

To see (
127u
) for (
62b
),
we recall (
128b
).
Multiplying this with
1/n^{q}
, applying
\ln()
and multiplying with
1/(1-q)

for
q<0
gives

\frac{q}{1-q}\ln(x_{\text{min}}/n)\leq R_{q}(\hat{\xi})\leq\frac{\ln({\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert})}{1-q}+\frac{q}{1-q}\ln(x_{\text{min}}/n)
.
Taking the limit
q\to-\infty
leads to the claim.

To see (
127v
), (
127w
), (
127x
) and (
127y
) for (
62b
),
note that
P_{q}=\mathrm{e}^{R_{q}}
is a monotone function of
R_{q}
, for which we have
(
127r
), (
127s
), (
127t
) and (
127u
).

D.3
q
-monotonicity of power based generator functions

Here we list the monotonicity of the power based generator functions
studied in Section
4

with respect to the parameter
q
.
Although many of these are more or less well known,
we collect these together with the proofs for the convenience of the reader.

\displaystyle q<q^{\prime}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;s_{q}(\hat{\xi})\leq s_{q^{\prime}}(\hat{\xi}),

(129a)

\displaystyle 0<q<q^{\prime}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;N_{q}(\hat{\xi})\geq N_{q^{\prime}}(\hat{\xi}),

(129b)

\displaystyle q<0<q^{\prime}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;N_{q}(\hat{\xi})\leq N_{q^{\prime}}(\hat{\xi}),

(129c)

\displaystyle q<q^{\prime}<0

\displaystyle\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;N_{q}(\hat{\xi})\geq N_{q^{\prime}}(\hat{\xi}),

(129d)

\displaystyle q<q^{\prime}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;M_{q}(\hat{\xi})\leq M_{q^{\prime}}(\hat{\xi}),

(129e)

\displaystyle q<q^{\prime}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;T_{q}(\hat{\xi})\geq T_{q^{\prime}}(\hat{\xi}),

(129f)

\displaystyle q<q^{\prime}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;R_{q}(\hat{\xi})\geq R_{q^{\prime}}(\hat{\xi}),

(129g)

\displaystyle q<q^{\prime}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;P_{q}(\hat{\xi})\geq P_{q^{\prime}}(\hat{\xi}).

(129h)

To see (
129a
) for (
59a
),
we have (
124
) for each summand,
1\leq x
.

To see (
129b
)-(
129d
) for (
59b
),
we note that
N_{q}(\hat{\xi})
is not continuous in
q=0
, see (
127e
)-(
127f
),
so first we consider the cases
0<q\leq q^{\prime}
and
q\leq q^{\prime}<0
.
For these, for
0\lessgtr q

we have
x\lesseqgtr N_{q}(\hat{\xi})
for all
x\in\hat{\xi}

(by (
125
), since
x^{q}\leq\sum_{x\in\hat{\xi}}x^{q}
),
so
x/N_{q}(\hat{\xi})\lesseqgtr 1
.
Then
\bigl(x/N_{q}(\hat{\xi})\bigr)^{q}\gtreqless\bigl(x/N_{q}(\hat{\xi})\bigr)^{q^{\prime}}

by (
124
).
Summing up,

1=\sum_{x\in\hat{\xi}}\bigl(x/N_{q}(\hat{\xi})\bigr)^{q}\gtreqless\sum_{x\in\hat{\xi}}\bigl(x/N_{q}(\hat{\xi})\bigr)^{q^{\prime}}
,
that is,

N_{q}^{q^{\prime}}(\hat{\xi})\gtreqless\sum_{x\in\hat{\xi}}x^{q^{\prime}}
,
which is

N_{q}(\hat{\xi})\geq N_{q^{\prime}}(\hat{\xi})

in the two cases
0\lessgtr q

by (
125
).
Now for the case
q<0<q^{\prime}
we have the limits (
127h
)-(
127i
),
by which
N_{q}\leq N_{-\infty}=\min\leq\max=N_{+\infty}\leq N_{q^{\prime}}
.

To see (
129e
) for (
59c
) (power mean inequality, with equal weights),
first we consider the cases
0<q\leq q^{\prime}
and
q\leq q^{\prime}<0
.
For these, for
0\lessgtr q
,
we have

0<q/q^{\prime}\lesseqgtr 1
, so the function

x\mapsto x^{q/q^{\prime}}
is concave/convex in the two cases,
so

\sum_{x\in\hat{\xi}}\frac{1}{{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}}x^{q}=\sum_{x\in\hat{\xi}}\frac{1}{{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}}(x^{q^{\prime}})^{q/q^{\prime}}\lesseqgtr\bigl(\sum_{x\in\hat{\xi}}\frac{1}{{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}}x^{q^{\prime}}\bigr)^{q/q^{\prime}}
,
which, taking the
q
-th root, is

M_{q}(\hat{\xi})\leq M_{q^{\prime}}(\hat{\xi})

by (
125
).
Now for the case
q<0<q^{\prime}
we have the limit (
127j
),
by which

\ln(M_{0}(\hat{\xi}))=\ln\bigl(\bigl(\prod_{x\in\hat{\xi}}x\bigr)^{1/{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}}\bigr)=\sum_{x\in\hat{\xi}}\frac{1}{{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}}\ln(x)=\frac{1}{q}\sum_{x\in\hat{\xi}}\frac{1}{{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}}\ln(x^{q})\lesseqgtr\frac{1}{q}\ln\bigl(\sum_{x\in\hat{\xi}}\frac{1}{{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}}x^{q}\bigr)=\ln\bigl(\bigl(\sum_{x\in\hat{\xi}}\frac{1}{{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}}x^{q}\bigr)^{1/q}\bigr)=\ln(M_{q}(\hat{\xi}))

for the cases
0\lesseqgtr q
,
where the concavity of the logarithm was used.

To see (
129f
) for (
62a
),
we differentiate the
q
-logarithm
\ln_{(q)}(u)=\frac{u^{1-q}-1}{1-q}
for
u>0
as

\frac{\partial}{\partial q}\ln_{(q)}(u)=\frac{1}{(1-q)^{2}}\bigl(u^{1-q}-1-(1-q)u^{1-q}\ln(u)\bigr)=\frac{1}{(1-q)^{2}}\bigl(u^{1-q}-1-u^{1-q}\ln(u^{1-q})\bigr)=\frac{1}{(1-q)^{2}}\bigl(v-1-v\ln(v)\bigr)\leq 0

using the new variable
v=u^{1-q}
.
This holds because
-v\ln(v)\leq 1-v
, which follows from that
the two sides, as well as their derivatives are equal in
v=1
,
and the derivative of the left-hand side (
-1-\ln(v)
) is larger/smaller than that of the right-hand side (
-1
)
for
v\leq 1
and
v\geq 1
, respectively.
So
\ln_{(q)}(u)
is decreasing in
q
,
then
T_{q}(\hat{\xi})=\frac{1}{1-q}\Bigl(\sum_{x\in\hat{\xi}}\bigl(\frac{x}{n}\bigr)^{q}-1\Bigr)=\sum_{x\in\hat{\xi}}\frac{x}{n}\ln_{(q)}\bigl(\frac{n}{x}\bigr)

is also decreasing in
q
.

To see (
129g
) for (
62b
),
we differentiate
R_{q}
as

\begin{split}&(1-q)^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial q}R_{q}(\hat{\xi})=(1-q)^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial q}\frac{1}{1-q}\ln\Bigl(\sum_{x\in\hat{\xi}}\frac{x^{q}}{n^{q}}\Bigr)\\
&\quad=\ln\Bigl(\sum_{x}\frac{x^{q}}{n^{q}}\Bigr)+\frac{(1-q)}{\sum_{x^{\prime}}\frac{{x^{\prime}}^{q}}{n^{q}}}\sum_{x}\frac{x^{q}}{n^{q}}\ln\bigl(\frac{x}{n}\bigr)\\
&\quad=\ln\Bigl(\sum_{x^{\prime\prime}}\frac{{x^{\prime\prime}}^{q}}{n^{q}}\Bigr)+\sum_{x}\frac{\frac{x^{q}}{n^{q}}}{\sum_{x^{\prime}}\frac{{x^{\prime}}^{q}}{n^{q}}}\Bigl(\ln\bigl(\frac{x}{n}\bigr)-\ln\bigl(\frac{x^{q}}{n^{q}}\bigr)\Bigr)\\
&\quad=\sum_{x}\frac{\frac{x^{q}}{n^{q}}}{\sum_{x^{\prime}}\frac{{x^{\prime}}^{q}}{n^{q}}}\Bigl(\ln\Bigl(\sum_{x^{\prime\prime}}\frac{{x^{\prime\prime}}^{q}}{n^{q}}\Bigr)+\ln\bigl(\frac{x}{n}\bigr)-\ln\bigl(\frac{x^{q}}{n^{q}}\bigr)\Bigr)\\
&\quad=-\sum_{x}\frac{\frac{x^{q}}{n^{q}}}{\sum_{x^{\prime}}\frac{{x^{\prime}}^{q}}{n^{q}}}\Bigl(\ln\Bigl(\frac{\frac{x^{q}}{n^{q}}}{\sum_{x^{\prime\prime}}\frac{{x^{\prime\prime}}^{q}}{n^{q}}}\Bigr)-\ln\bigl(\frac{x}{n}\bigr)\Bigr),\end{split}

so
-(1-q)^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial q}R_{q}(\hat{\xi}

is the Kullback-Leibler divergence
D(p,r)=\sum_{i}p_{i}(\ln(p_{i})-\ln(q_{i}))
, or relative entropy
[
114
]
of the distributions

\bigl\{\frac{(x_{i}/n)^{q}}{s_{q}(\hat{\xi}/n)}\;\big|\;i=1,2,\dots,{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}\bigr\}

and

\hat{\xi}/n=\{x_{i}/n\;|\;i=1,2,\dots,{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}\}

(for any indexing of the elements
\hat{\xi}
).
This is nonnegative,
which can be seen by the convexity of the logarithm:
-D(p,r)=\sum_{i}p_{i}\ln(\frac{q_{i}}{p_{i}})\leq\ln(\sum_{i}p_{i}\frac{q_{i}}{p_{i}}=\ln(\sum_{i}q_{i})=0
.

To see (
129h
) for (
63
),
note that
P_{q}=\mathrm{e}^{R_{q}}
is a strictly increasing function of
R_{q}
,
for which we have (
129g
).

Appendix E
Construction of
f
-entanglement

In this section we provide some tools for the refinement and dominance orders used in the main text,
and show the monotonicity of the generator functions (
22
) in Section
4

with respect to these.

E.1
Orders

Here we recall the partial orders
applied in the construction in the main text
[
18
,
20
]
,
and consider their relationship.

An
extremal stochastic
m^{\prime}\times m
matrix
is given
as a matrix of elements
a_{ij}\in\{0,1\}
,
such that
\sum_{i=1}^{m^{\prime}}a_{ij}=1
for all
j=1,2,\dots,m
.

A
partition
of the set
\{1,2,\dots,n\}
is its disjoint covering

\xi=\{X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{{\lvert\xi\rvert}}\}
,
and the elements
X\in\xi
are called
parts
.
The set of partitions of
\{1,2,\dots,n\}
is denoted with
{P}_{\text{I}}
.
The partial order
refinement

[
55
,
48
]
is given for the partitions
\upsilon
and
\xi
as

\upsilon\preceq\xi\;\;\overset{\text{def.}}{\Longleftrightarrow}\;\;\forall Y\in\upsilon,\exists X\in\xi\;\;\text{s.t.}\;\;Y\subseteq X.

(130a)

It is easy to check that for the partitions

\upsilon=\{Y_{1},Y_{2},\dots,Y_{{\lvert\upsilon\rvert}}\}
and
\xi=\{X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{{\lvert\xi\rvert}}\}

we have

\begin{split}\upsilon\preceq\xi\;\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\;\text{$\exists[a_{ij}]$ extremal stochastic}\\
\text{${\lvert\xi\rvert}\times{\lvert\upsilon\rvert}$ matrix, s.t. }X_{i}=\bigcup_{j=1}^{{\lvert\upsilon\rvert}}{a_{ij}}Y_{j},\end{split}

(130b)

(where we use the notation
0Y=\emptyset
,
1Y=Y
),
which informally means that

\xi>\upsilon
can be obtained from
\upsilon

by forming partial unions.
Note that the set partitions
P_{\text{I}}
with respect to the refinement order forms a lattice
[
116
,
48
]
,
that is, there exist unique least upper and greatest lower bounds for all pairs of set partitions.
The covering relation (arrow) of
\preceq
is

\upsilon\mathrel{\mathchoice{{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to7.7778pt{\hss$\cdot$}\hss}\hbox{$\prec$}}}{{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to7.7778pt{\hss$\cdot$}\hss}\hbox{$\prec$}}}{\scriptstyle{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to6.25002pt{\hss$\cdot$}\hss}\hbox{$\prec$}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.41673pt{\hss$\cdot$}\hss}\hbox{$\prec$}}}}\xi\;\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\;\upsilon\prec\xi\;\text{and}\;{\lvert\upsilon\rvert}={\lvert\xi\rvert}-1,

(130c)

which informally means that
only two parts from
\upsilon
are joined to obtain
\hat{\xi}
.

The
type

\hat{\xi}
of a partition
\xi
is given as the
multiset

\hat{\xi}=\{{\lvert X\rvert}\;|\;X\in\xi\}
, which is an integer partition of
n

[
55
,
48
,
20
]
.
Let
s
denote the elementwise action of
{\lvert\;\rvert}
,
so
\hat{\xi}=s(\xi)
, and
let
s^{-1}
denote its inverse image,

s^{-1}(\hat{\xi})=\{\xi\in P_{\text{I}}\;|\;s(\xi)=\hat{\xi}\}
.
The set of integer partitions of
n
is denoted with
\hat{P}_{\text{I}}
.
The partial order
refinement

[
20
]
is given for the integer partitions
\hat{\upsilon}
and
\hat{\xi}
as

\hat{\upsilon}\preceq\hat{\xi}\;\;\overset{\text{def.}}{\Longleftrightarrow}\;\;\exists\upsilon\in s^{-1}(\hat{\upsilon}),\xi\in s^{-1}(\hat{\xi})\;\;\text{s.t.}\;\;\upsilon\preceq\xi.

(131a)

It is easy to check that for the integer partitions

\hat{\upsilon}=\{y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{{\lvert\hat{\upsilon}\rvert}}\}
and
\hat{\xi}=\{x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}}\}

we have

\begin{split}\hat{\upsilon}\preceq\hat{\xi}\;\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\;\text{$\exists[a_{ij}]$ extremal stochastic}\\
\text{${\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}\times{\lvert\hat{\upsilon}\rvert}$ matrix, s.t. }x_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\upsilon}\rvert}}a_{ij}y_{j},\end{split}

(131b)

which informally means that

\hat{\xi}>\hat{\upsilon}
can be obtained from
\hat{\upsilon}

by partial summation.
Note that the integer partitions
\hat{P}_{\text{I}}
with respect to the refinement order does not form a lattice,
that is, there are pairs of integer partitions for which there exists no unique least upper or greatest lower bound
(for
n\geq 5
, see Figure
1
).
The covering relation (arrow, see Figure
1
) of
\preceq
is

\hat{\upsilon}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to7.7778pt{\hss$\cdot$}\hss}\hbox{$\prec$}}}{{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to7.7778pt{\hss$\cdot$}\hss}\hbox{$\prec$}}}{\scriptstyle{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to6.25002pt{\hss$\cdot$}\hss}\hbox{$\prec$}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.41673pt{\hss$\cdot$}\hss}\hbox{$\prec$}}}}\hat{\xi}\;\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\;\hat{\upsilon}\prec\hat{\xi}\;\text{and}\;{\lvert\hat{\upsilon}\rvert}={\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}-1,

(131c)

which informally means that
only two parts from
\hat{\upsilon}
are added to obtain
\hat{\xi}
.

Note that, although the definition of extremal stochastic matrices allows
0
rows,
such matrices do not play role in the characterizations (
130b
) and (
131b
).

Figure 10:
The covering relations of the refinement poset (black arrows, (
130c
))
and the dominance lattice (dashed red arrows, (
132b
)) on
\hat{P}_{\text{I}}

illustrated for
n=6
in the height (
23a
) vs. width (
23b
) plot.

The
dominance order
(also called
majorization

[
87
,
88
,
89
]
for the continuous variable case),
is a partial order given for the integer partitions
[
90
,
48
]

\hat{\upsilon}=\{y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{{\lvert\hat{\upsilon}\rvert}}\}
and
\hat{\xi}=\{x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}}\}
,
indexed in weakly decreasing order (
x_{i}\geq x_{j}
and
y_{i}\geq y_{j}
,
i<j
), as

\begin{split}\hat{\upsilon}\leq\hat{\xi}\;\;\overset{\text{def.}}{\Longleftrightarrow}\;\;&\sum_{i=1}^{m}y_{i}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{m}x_{i}\\
&\quad\text{for all $m\in\{1,2,\dots,n\}$},\end{split}

(132a)

where
y_{i}=0
or
x_{i}=0
is set if
i>{\lvert\hat{\upsilon}\rvert}
or
i>{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}
, respectively.
Note that the integer partitions
\hat{P}_{\text{I}}
with respect to the dominance order forms a lattice
[
90
]
,
that is, there exist unique least upper and greatest lower bounds for all pairs of integer partitions.
The covering relation (arrow) of
\leq
is also known
[
90
]
,

\hat{\upsilon}\lessdot\hat{\xi}\;\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\;\begin{cases}x_{i}=y_{i}+1,\\
x_{j}=y_{j}-1,\\
x_{k}=y_{k},\forall k\neq i,j,\\
\text{when $j=i+1$ or $y_{i}=y_{j}$}\end{cases}

(132b)

which informally means that
the Young diagram of
\hat{\xi}\gtrdot\hat{\upsilon}

can be obtained from the Young diagram of
\hat{\upsilon}

by removing the last box of row
j
and appending it
either to the end of the immediately preceding row
j-1
,
or to the end of row
i<j
if the rows
i
through
j
of the Young diagram of
\hat{\upsilon}
all have the same length,

y_{i-1}<y_{i}=y_{i+1}=\dots=y_{j-1}=y_{j}<y_{j+1}
.
(Note that we drop the possible
x_{j}=y_{j}-1=0
values.)
(For illustration, see Figure
10
.)
We will also use the following condition

\hat{\upsilon}\leq\hat{\xi}\;\;\Longleftarrow\;\;\exists i<j\;\text{s.t.}\;\begin{cases}x_{i}=y_{i}+1,\\
x_{j}=y_{j}-1,\\
x_{k}=y_{k},\forall k\neq i,j,\end{cases}

(132c)

which is easy to prove by the definition (
132a
).

Now we show that
refinement
\preceq
implies dominance
\leq
,
that is, for the integer partitions
\hat{\upsilon},\hat{\xi}\in\hat{P}_{\text{I}}
,

\hat{\upsilon}\preceq\hat{\xi}\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;\hat{\upsilon}\leq\hat{\xi}.

(133)

To see this, we assume (
131b
)
with weakly decreasingly ordered elements
y_{j}
and
x_{i}
.
Before we show the inequality in (
132a
) for general
m
,
first let us check the
m=1
and
2
cases as illustration.
For the case
m=1
,
let
i\in\{1,2,\dots,{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}\}
be the index for which
a_{i1}=1

(this is
unique
, because
a_{ij}
are elements of an extremal stochastic matrix),
then

y_{1}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\upsilon}\rvert}}a_{ij}y_{j}=x_{i}\leq x_{1},

where the assumption (
131b
) and the decreasing ordering of the elements
x_{i}
are also used.
For the case
m=2
,
let
i_{1},i_{2}\in\{1,2,\dots,{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}\}
be the
unique
indices for which
a_{i_{1}1}=1
and
a_{i_{2}2}=1
,
then

y_{1}+y_{2}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\upsilon}\rvert}}a_{i_{1}j}y_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\upsilon}\rvert}}a_{i_{2}j}y_{j}=x_{i_{1}}+x_{i_{2}}\leq x_{1}+x_{2},

where the assumption (
131b
) and the decreasing ordering of the elements
x_{i}
are also used:
the sum of any two
x
s are less than or equal to the sum of the two largest ones.
In general, for all
l\in\{1,2,\dots,m\}
,
let
i_{l}\in\{1,2,\dots,{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}\}
be the
unique
indices for which
a_{i_{l}l}=1
,
then

\sum_{l=1}^{m}y_{l}\leq\sum_{l=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\upsilon}\rvert}}a_{i_{l}j}y_{j}=\sum_{l=1}^{m}x_{i_{l}}\leq\sum_{l=1}^{m}x_{l},

where the assumption (
131b
) and the decreasing ordering of the elements
x_{i}
are also used:
the sum of any
l

x
-s are less than or equal to the sum of the
l
largest ones.
Then (
132a
) leads to the claim.
Note that the reverse implication does not hold in (
133
),
as can be shown by the simple counterexample

\hat{\upsilon}=\{2,2\}
and
\hat{\xi}=\{3,1\}
,
for which
\hat{\upsilon}\leq\hat{\xi}

but
\hat{\upsilon}\not\preceq\hat{\xi}
and
\hat{\upsilon}\not\succeq\hat{\xi}
.

There are functions
f:\hat{P}_{\text{I}}\to\mathbb{R}

increasing/decreasing monotone with respect to dominance (
132a
)

\hat{\upsilon}\leq\hat{\xi}\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;f(\hat{\upsilon})\lesseqgtr f(\hat{\xi}).

(134)

(These are called
Schur-convex/concave
for the case of probability distributions or continuous variables in general.)
Then, putting together (
22b
) and (
134
) with (
133
),
we have that any
\leq
-monotone (dominance-monotone) function
\hat{P}_{\text{I}}\to\mathbb{R}

is
\preceq
-monotone (refinement-monotone),
hence proper generator function (
22
).
In the continuous variable case, decreasing dominance-monotonicity,
or Schur-concavity is the defining property of
(generalized) entropies

[
87
]
,
so any entropy of the normalized integer partitions
\hat{\xi}/n\equiv\{x/n\;|\;x\in\hat{\xi}\}

is a proper generator function.
Note, however, that for integer partitions, as is being considered here,
we have a slightly different domain of the functions.
Usually integer partitions of
n
are represented as
n
-tuples of nonnegative integers
\{0,1,2,\dots,n\}\subset\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
by adding zeroes

\hat{\xi}=\{x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}}\}\mapsto(x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}},0,\dots,0)

and then the dominance-monotonicity or Schur-convexity/concavity of
n
-variable functions over
\{0,1,2,\dots,n\}^{n}
or
[0,n]_{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
can be defined.
Here we represent partitions of
n
as multisets of strictly positive integers
\{1,2,\dots,n\}\subset\mathbb{N}
,
so zero is not allowed, which is the natural way in our construction.
Then the generator functions acting on partitions
can be valid even if the analogously defined
n
-variable functions would not
(see for example the power based generator functions
s_{q}
,
N_{q}
,
M_{q}
,
T_{q}
,
R_{q}
,
P_{q}
for
q<0
in Appendix
E.2
).
This also prevents the use of the Schur-Ostrowski criterion
[
88
]
for
n
-variable functions
to check Schur-convexity/concavity,
so we will give direct proofs (see in Appendix
E.3
) based on the covering relation (
132b
).

E.2
Monotonicity of generator functions

Here we show the monotonicity properties of the generator functions
studied in Section
4
.

\displaystyle m\in\mathbb{N}:\quad\hat{\upsilon}\preceq\hat{\xi}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;w_{m}(\hat{\upsilon})\leq w_{m}(\hat{\xi}),

(135a)

\displaystyle m\in\mathbb{N}:\quad\hat{\upsilon}\preceq\hat{\xi}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;t_{m}(\hat{\upsilon})\leq t_{m}(\hat{\xi}),

(135b)

\displaystyle 1\lesseqgtr q:\quad\hat{\upsilon}\preceq\hat{\xi}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;s_{q}(\hat{\upsilon})\lesseqgtr s_{q}(\hat{\xi}),

(135c)

\displaystyle\left.\begin{aligned} q<0,1\leq q\\
0<q\leq 1\end{aligned}\right\}\quad\hat{\upsilon}\preceq\hat{\xi}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;N_{q}(\hat{\upsilon})\lesseqgtr N_{q}(\hat{\xi}),

(135d)

\displaystyle 1\leq q:\quad\hat{\upsilon}\preceq\hat{\xi}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;M_{q}(\hat{\upsilon})\leq M_{q}(\hat{\xi}),

(135e)

\displaystyle q\in\mathbb{R}:\quad\hat{\upsilon}\preceq\hat{\xi}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;T_{q}(\hat{\upsilon})\geq T_{q}(\hat{\xi}),

(135f)

\displaystyle q\in\mathbb{R},\pm\infty:\quad\hat{\upsilon}\preceq\hat{\xi}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;R_{q}(\hat{\upsilon})\geq R_{q}(\hat{\xi}),

(135g)

\displaystyle\hat{\upsilon}\preceq\hat{\xi}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;S(\hat{\upsilon})\geq S(\hat{\xi}),

(135h)

\displaystyle q\in\mathbb{R},\pm\infty:\quad\hat{\upsilon}\preceq\hat{\xi}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;P_{q}(\hat{\upsilon})\geq P_{q}(\hat{\xi}),

(135i)

\displaystyle\left.\begin{aligned} 2\leq b\\
0<b\leq 1\end{aligned}\right\}\quad\hat{\upsilon}\preceq\hat{\xi}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;\Dim_{b}(\hat{\upsilon})\lesseqgtr\Dim_{b}(\hat{\xi}),

(135j)

\displaystyle 0<b:\quad\hat{\upsilon}\preceq\hat{\xi}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;\Dim^{\prime}_{b}(\hat{\upsilon})\leq\Dim^{\prime}_{b}(\hat{\xi}),

(135k)

\displaystyle\left.\begin{aligned} 0<b<1\\
2\leq b\end{aligned}\right\}\quad\hat{\upsilon}\preceq\hat{\xi}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;\DoF_{b}(\hat{\upsilon})\leq\DoF_{b}(\hat{\xi}),

(135l)

\displaystyle 1<b:\quad\hat{\upsilon}\preceq\hat{\xi}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;\DoF^{\prime}_{b}(\hat{\upsilon})\leq\DoF^{\prime}_{b}(\hat{\xi}).

(135m)

Note that for the entanglement dimension (
67a
)-(
67b
)
and the entanglement degree of freedom (
68a
)-(
68b
)
we considered more general parameter ranges
b\in\mathbb{R}
for the sake of mathematical completeness.
These functions bear physical motivation for
b=d\in\{2,3,4,\dots\}
, being Hilbert space dimensions.

To see (
135a
) for (
57a
),
note that
w_{m}
is the sum of the largest
m
parts,
and
w_{m}(\hat{\upsilon})\leq w_{m}(\hat{\xi})
for all
m
is just the condition (
132a
) for dominance,
which has already been shown to follow from the refinement (
133
).

To see (
135b
) for (
57b
),
let us have
\hat{\xi}=\{x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}}\}

and
\hat{\upsilon}=\{y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{{\lvert\hat{\upsilon}\rvert}}\}

with increasingly ordered indexing,
x_{i}\leq x_{j}
and
y_{i}\leq y_{j}
for
i<j
.
Then
t_{m}
is the sum of the
m
smallest parts,
which for
\hat{\upsilon}\preceq\hat{\xi}
is

t_{m}(\hat{\xi})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}x_{i}\overset{\eqref{eq:poIp.x}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sum_{j=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\upsilon}\rvert}}a_{ij}y_{j}\geq\sum_{j=1}^{m}y_{j}=t_{m}(\hat{\upsilon}),

where the inequality follows from that on the left-hand side the number of summands is at least
m
,
while on the right-hand side there is the sum of the
m
smallest parts.
(Here
x_{i}
and
y_{j}
are set to zero if
i>{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}
and
j>{\lvert\hat{\upsilon}\rvert}
, respectively.)
Note that, contrary to the proof of (
135a
),
here we could not use the majorization,
since zeroes are not allowed in this formalism.

To see (
135c
) for (
59a
),

\begin{split}&s_{q}(\hat{\xi})\overset{\eqref{eq:sq}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{i=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}}x_{i}^{q}\overset{\eqref{eq:poIp.x}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{i=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}}\Bigl(\sum_{j=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\upsilon}\rvert}}a_{ij}y_{j}\Bigr)^{q}\\
&\quad{\;\;=\;\;}\sum_{i=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}}\Bigl(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1,\\
a_{ij}\neq 0\end{subarray}}^{{\lvert\hat{\upsilon}\rvert}}y_{j}\Bigr)^{q}\overset{\eqref{eq:sumq}}{{\;\;\gtreqless\;\;}}\sum_{i=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1,\\
a_{ij}\neq 0\end{subarray}}^{{\lvert\hat{\upsilon}\rvert}}y_{j}^{q}\\
&\quad{\;\;=\;\;}\sum_{i=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}}\sum_{j=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\upsilon}\rvert}}a_{ij}y_{j}^{q}{\;\;=\;\;}\sum_{j=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\upsilon}\rvert}}\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}}a_{ij}\Bigr)y_{j}^{q}\\
&\quad{\;\;=\;\;}\sum_{j=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\upsilon}\rvert}}y_{j}^{q}\overset{\eqref{eq:sq}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}s_{q}(\hat{\upsilon}),\end{split}

where at the unlabeled equalities we used the properties of the extremal stochastic matrix,
arising from (
131b
) by the assumption.

To see (
135d
) for (
59b
), note that
the (
135c
) monotonicity of
s_{q}
is flipped
by the monotonicity of
u\mapsto u^{1/q}
if
q<0
, see (
125
).

To see (
135e
) for (
59c
), note that

{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}=s_{0}(\hat{\xi})
is decreasing (
135c
),
so
1/s_{0}
is increasing,

s_{q}
is increasing for
1\leq q
(
135c
),
so
s_{q}/s_{0}
is increasing,
so
M_{q}
is increasing for
1\leq q
(
59c
)
by the monotonicity of
u\mapsto u^{1/q}
, see (
125
).
We note that
counterexamples show that
M_{q}
is not monotone for
q<1
.

To see (
135g
) for (
62b
), note that

u\mapsto\ln(u)
is increasing monotone,
so
\hat{\xi}\mapsto\ln(s_{q}(\hat{\xi}))
is increasing/decreasing for
1\lesseqgtr q
by (
135c
);
on the other hand,

0\gtrless\frac{1}{1-q}
for
1\lessgtr q
,
so the multiplication with that flips the monotonicity for
1<q
,
leading to the claim for
q\neq 1
,
which also holds for
q=1
by the continuity of
q\mapsto R_{q}
, (
127s
).

We have (
135f
) for (
62a
)
by similar reasoning as for (
135g
).

We have (
135h
) for (
62c
),
since this is just the
q=1
case
of (
135f
) by (
127o
) or
of (
135g
) by (
127s
).

To see (
135i
) for (
63
), note that
the (
135g
) monotonicity of
R_{q}
is not changed
by the monotonicity of
u\mapsto\mathrm{e}^{u}
.

To see (
135j
) for (
67a
),

\begin{split}&\Dim_{b}(\hat{\xi})\overset{\eqref{eq:Dimd}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{i=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}}b^{x_{i}}\overset{\eqref{eq:poIp.x}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{i=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}}b^{\sum_{j=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\upsilon}\rvert}}a_{ij}y_{j}}\\
&\quad\overset{\eqref{eq:ssadd}}{{\;\;\gtreqless\;\;}}\sum_{i=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}}\sum_{j=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\upsilon}\rvert}}a_{ij}b^{y_{j}}{\;\;=\;\;}\sum_{j=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\upsilon}\rvert}}\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}}a_{ij}\Bigr)b^{y_{j}}\\
&\quad{\;\;=\;\;}\sum_{j=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\upsilon}\rvert}}b^{y_{j}}\overset{\eqref{eq:Dimd}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Dim_{b}(\hat{\upsilon}),\end{split}

where at the unlabeled equalities we used the properties of the extremal stochastic matrix,
arising from (
131b
) by the assumption.
The inequality is from the super/subadditivity of the exponential function for this case

b^{\sum_{j=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\upsilon}\rvert}}a_{ij}y_{j}}{\;\;\gtreqless\;\;}\sum_{j=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\upsilon}\rvert}}a_{ij}b^{y_{j}},

(136)

which holds if and only if

b^{z_{1}+z_{2}}{\;\;\gtreqless\;\;}b^{z_{1}}+b^{z_{2}}

for any
1\leq z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
,
which is equivalent to

1{\;\;\gtreqless\;\;}1/b^{z_{1}}+1/b^{z_{2}}
.
If
2\leq b
, we have
1\geq 1/b^{z_{1}}+1/b^{z_{2}}
,
since it holds for
b=2
,
z_{1}=z_{2}=1
, and the right-hand side
is decreasing with
z_{1},z_{2}
, see (
124
),
and decreasing with
b
, see (
125
);
if
0<b\leq 1
, we have
1\leq 1/b^{z_{1}}+1/b^{z_{2}}
,
since it holds for
b=1
,
z_{1}=z_{2}=1
, and the right-hand side
is increasing with
z_{1},z_{2}
, see (
124
),
and increasing with the decreasing of
b
, see (
125
);
for the intermediate case
1<b<2
, both inequalities can be violated for suitable values of
z_{1},z_{2}
.

To see (
135k
) for (
67b
),

\displaystyle\Dim^{\prime}_{b}(\hat{\xi})\overset{\eqref{eq:Dimpd}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{i=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}}(b^{x_{i}}-1)+1

\displaystyle\quad\overset{\eqref{eq:poIp.x}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\sum_{i=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}}(b^{\sum_{j=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\upsilon}\rvert}}a_{ij}y_{j}}-1)+1

\displaystyle\quad\overset{\eqref{eq:ssaddp}}{{\;\;\leq\;\;}}\sum_{i=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}}\sum_{j=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\upsilon}\rvert}}a_{ij}(b^{y_{j}}-1)+1

\displaystyle\quad{\;\;=\;\;}\sum_{j=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\upsilon}\rvert}}\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}}a_{ij}\Bigr)(b^{y_{j}}-1)+1

\displaystyle\quad{\;\;=\;\;}\sum_{j=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\upsilon}\rvert}}(b^{y_{j}}-1)+1\overset{\eqref{eq:Dimpd}}{{\;\;=\;\;}}\Dim^{\prime}_{b}(\hat{\upsilon}),

where at the unlabeled equalities we used the properties of the extremal stochastic matrix,
arising from (
131b
) by the assumption.
The inequality is from the superadditivity of the modified exponential function

b^{\sum_{j=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\upsilon}\rvert}}a_{ij}y_{j}}-1\geq\sum_{j=1}^{{\lvert\hat{\upsilon}\rvert}}a_{ij}(b^{y_{j}}-1),

(137)

which holds if and only if

(b^{z_{1}+z_{2}}-1)\geq(b^{z_{1}}-1)+(b^{z_{2}}-1)

for any
1\leq z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
,
which is equivalent to

0\leq(b^{z_{1}}-1)(b^{z_{2}}-1)
,
which holds in both the
0<b\leq 1
and
1\leq b
cases.
(Note that the monotonicity for
2\leq b
would simply follow
from that of
\Dim_{b}
and
h
by definition (
67b
),
however, the direct proof here works for the larger range
0<b
.)

To see (
135l
) for (
68a
),
note that
u\mapsto\log_{b}(u)
is increasing/decreasing monotone for
1\lessgtr b
,
which flips the monotonicity of
\Dim_{b}
for which we have (
135j
).

To see (
135m
) for (
68b
),
note that
u\mapsto\log_{b}(u)
is increasing for
1<b
,
and for
\Dim^{\prime}_{b}
we have (
135k
).
(Note that the parameter range is constrained not to substitute negative number into the logarithm.
Since
\Dim^{\prime}_{b}
is increasing monotone (
135k
),
we need that
0\leq\Dim^{\prime}_{b}(\bot)=n(b-1)+1
.
This holds for
b\geq 1
,
but it is violated for
0<b<1
for
n\geq 1/(1-b)
.)

E.3
Dominance-monotonicity of generator functions

Here we show the monotonicity (
134
) of the generator functions, studied in Section
4
,
with respect to the dominance order.

\displaystyle m\in\mathbb{N}:\quad\hat{\upsilon}\leq\hat{\xi}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;w_{m}(\hat{\upsilon})\leq w_{m}(\hat{\xi}),

(138a)

\displaystyle\hat{\upsilon}\leq\hat{\xi}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;h(\hat{\upsilon})\geq h(\hat{\xi}),

(138b)

\displaystyle\hat{\upsilon}\leq\hat{\xi}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;r(\hat{\upsilon})\leq r(\hat{\xi}),

(138c)

\displaystyle\left.\begin{aligned} 1\leq q\\
0\leq q\leq 1\end{aligned}\right\}\quad\hat{\upsilon}\leq\hat{\xi}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;s_{q}(\hat{\upsilon})\lesseqgtr s_{q}(\hat{\xi}),

(138d)

\displaystyle\left.\begin{aligned} 1\leq q\\
0<q\leq 1\end{aligned}\right\}\quad\hat{\upsilon}\leq\hat{\xi}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;N_{q}(\hat{\upsilon})\lesseqgtr N_{q}(\hat{\xi}),

(138e)

\displaystyle 1\leq q:\quad\hat{\upsilon}\leq\hat{\xi}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;M_{q}(\hat{\upsilon})\leq M_{q}(\hat{\xi}),

(138f)

\displaystyle 0\leq q:\quad\hat{\upsilon}\leq\hat{\xi}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;T_{q}(\hat{\upsilon})\geq T_{q}(\hat{\xi}),

(138g)

\displaystyle 0\leq q,q=\infty:\quad\hat{\upsilon}\leq\hat{\xi}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;R_{q}(\hat{\upsilon})\geq R_{q}(\hat{\xi}),

(138h)

\displaystyle\hat{\upsilon}\leq\hat{\xi}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;S(\hat{\upsilon})\geq S(\hat{\xi}),

(138i)

\displaystyle 0\leq q,q=\infty:\quad\hat{\upsilon}\leq\hat{\xi}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;P_{q}(\hat{\upsilon})\geq P_{q}(\hat{\xi}),

(138j)

\displaystyle 2\leq b:\quad\hat{\upsilon}\leq\hat{\xi}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;\Dim_{b}(\hat{\upsilon})\leq\Dim_{b}(\hat{\xi}),

(138k)

\displaystyle 0<b:\quad\hat{\upsilon}\leq\hat{\xi}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;\Dim^{\prime}_{b}(\hat{\upsilon})\leq\Dim^{\prime}_{b}(\hat{\xi}),

(138l)

\displaystyle 2\leq b:\quad\hat{\upsilon}\leq\hat{\xi}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;\DoF_{b}(\hat{\upsilon})\leq\DoF_{b}(\hat{\xi}),

(138m)

\displaystyle 1<b:\quad\hat{\upsilon}\leq\hat{\xi}

\displaystyle\;\;\Longrightarrow\;\;\DoF^{\prime}_{b}(\hat{\upsilon})\leq\DoF^{\prime}_{b}(\hat{\xi}).

(138n)

Note that the dominance-monotonicity
is enough to be proven for arrows (
132b
),
however, sometimes it is easier to use more general steps,
for which the right-hand side of (
132c
) holds,
since these include the arrows.
Note that throughout this section
the integer partitions
\hat{\upsilon}=\{y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{{\lvert\hat{\upsilon}\rvert}}\}
and
\hat{\xi}=\{x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}}\}

are indexed in weakly decreasing order (
x_{i}\geq x_{j}
and
y_{i}\geq y_{j}
,
i<j
).

To see (
138a
) for (
57a
),
note that
w_{m}
is the sum of the largest
m
parts,
and
w_{m}(\hat{\upsilon})\leq w_{m}(\hat{\xi})
for all
m

is just the condition (
132a
) for the dominance order.

To see (
138b
) for (
23a
),
we need to check the monotonicity of
s_{q}

with respect to the steps (
132c
) of the dominance order.
For the case
2\leq y_{j}
, we have
{\lvert\hat{\upsilon}\rvert}={\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}
,
and for the case
y_{j}=1
, we have
{\lvert\hat{\upsilon}\rvert}={\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}+1
.

We have (
138c
) for (
23c
),
since rank is the difference of the width and the height,
being dominance-increasing (
138a
) and dominance-decreasing (
138b
), respectively.

To see (
138d
) for (
59a
),
we need to check the monotonicity of
s_{q}

with respect to the steps (
132c
) of the dominance order.
Writing
y:=y_{j}
and
\delta:=y_{i}-y_{j}\in\{0,1,2,\dots\}
, this is

\displaystyle(y+\delta)^{q}+y^{q}

\displaystyle\lesseqgtr(y+\delta+1)^{q}+(y-1)^{q},\quad\text{for $2\leq y$},

(139a)

\displaystyle(1+\delta)^{q}+1^{q}

\displaystyle\lesseqgtr(2+\delta)^{q},

(139b)

where we had to treat the
y=1
case separately,
since such a step results in
x_{j}=y_{j}-1=y-1=0
row (
132c
),
for which we have different form of the generator function.

First
, consider (
139a
),
which is equivalent to
y^{q}-(y-1)^{q}\lesseqgtr(y+(\delta+1))^{q}-(y+(\delta+1)-1)^{q}=(y+\Delta)^{q}-(y+\Delta-1)^{q}
,
which follows from the monotonicity of
\tau_{q}(y):=y^{q}-(y-1)^{q}
in
y
.
This is by
0\lesseqgtr(\partial\tau_{q}/\partial y)(y)=q(y^{q-1}-(y-1)^{q-1})
,
which is nonnegative for
q\leq 0
and
1\leq q
,
and nonpositive for
0\leq q\leq 1
by (
125
).

Second
, note that we do not need to check (
139b
) directly,
since the
y\equiv y_{j}=1
case means a step
where the only one box of the
j
-th row of
\hat{\upsilon}
is moved to the end of the
i
-th (
132c
),
so
x_{j}=y_{j}-1=0

and
x_{i}=y_{i}+1=y_{i}+y_{j}
,
that is,
\hat{\xi}
is obtained by partial summation of
\hat{\upsilon}
,
which is an arrow of the refinement (
131c
),
\hat{\upsilon}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to7.7778pt{\hss$\cdot$}\hss}\hbox{$\prec$}}}{{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to7.7778pt{\hss$\cdot$}\hss}\hbox{$\prec$}}}{\scriptstyle{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to6.25002pt{\hss$\cdot$}\hss}\hbox{$\prec$}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.41673pt{\hss$\cdot$}\hss}\hbox{$\prec$}}}}\hat{\xi}
,
for which we have the monotonicity (
135c
).

Finally
, the two cases agree for
0\leq q
, where

s_{q}
is increasing/decreasing with respect to dominance for
1\leq q
or
0\leq q\leq 1
, respectively,
and we do not have dominance-monotonicity for
q<0
.

To see (
138e
) for (
59b
), note that
the (
138d
) dominance-monotonicity of
s_{q}
is not changed
by the monotonicity of
u\mapsto u^{1/q}
if
0<q
, see (
125
).

To see (
138f
) for (
59c
), note that

{\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}=s_{0}(\hat{\xi})
is dominance-decreasing (
138d
),
so
1/s_{0}
is dominance-increasing,

s_{q}
is dominance-increasing for
1\leq q
(
138d
),
so
s_{q}/s_{0}
is dominance-increasing,
so
M_{q}
is dominance-increasing for
1\leq q
(
59c
)
by the monotonicity of
u\mapsto u^{1/q}
, see (
125
).
We have already seen that
M_{q}
is not even a generator function for
q<1
, (
135e
),
so cannot be dominance-monotone (
133
) either.

To see (
138h
) for (
62b
), note that

u\mapsto\ln(u)
is increasing monotone,
so in the range
0\leq q
,
\hat{\xi}\mapsto\ln(s_{q}(\hat{\xi}))
is dominance-increasing/decreasing for
1\lesseqgtr q
by (
138d
);
on the other hand,

0\gtrless\frac{1}{1-q}
for
1\lessgtr q
,
so the multiplication with that flips the dominance-monotonicity for
1<q
,
leading to the claim for
q\neq 1
,
which also holds for
q=1
by the continuity of
q\mapsto R_{q}
, (
127s
).

We have (
138g
) for (
62a
)
by similar reasoning as for (
138h
).

We have (
138i
) for (
62c
),
since this is just the
q=1
case
of (
138g
) by (
127o
) or
of (
138h
) by (
127s
).

To see (
138j
) for (
63
), note that
the (
138h
) dominance-monotonicity of
R_{q}
is not changed
by the monotonicity of
u\mapsto\mathrm{e}^{u}
.

To see (
138k
) for (
67a
),
we need to check the monotonicity of
\Dim_{b}

with respect to the steps (
132c
) of the dominance order.
Writing
y:=y_{j}
and
\delta:=y_{i}-y_{j}\in\{0,1,2,\dots\}
, this is

\displaystyle b^{y+\delta}+b^{y}

\displaystyle\lesseqgtr b^{y+\delta+1}+b^{y-1},\quad\text{for $2\leq y$},

(140a)

\displaystyle b^{1+\delta}+b

\displaystyle\lesseqgtr b^{2+\delta},

(140b)

where we had to treat the
y=1
case separately,
since such a step results in
x_{j}=y_{j}-1=y-1=0
row (
132c
),
for which we have different form of the generator function.

First,
consider (
140a
),
which is equivalent to
0\lesseqgtr(b-1)(b^{y+\delta}-b^{y-1})=b^{y-1}(b-1)(b^{\delta+1}-1)
,
which is nonnegative for
0<b
, since the two parentheses are positive or negative simultaneously.

Second,
note that we do not need to check (
140b
) directly,
since the
y\equiv y_{j}=1
case means a step
where the only one box of the
j
-th row of
\hat{\upsilon}
is moved to the end of the
i
-th (
132c
),
so
x_{j}=y_{j}-1=0

and
x_{i}=y_{i}+1=y_{i}+y_{j}
,
that is,
\hat{\xi}
is obtained by partial summation of
\hat{\upsilon}
,
which is an arrow of the refinement (
131c
),
\hat{\upsilon}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to7.7778pt{\hss$\cdot$}\hss}\hbox{$\prec$}}}{{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to7.7778pt{\hss$\cdot$}\hss}\hbox{$\prec$}}}{\scriptstyle{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to6.25002pt{\hss$\cdot$}\hss}\hbox{$\prec$}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.41673pt{\hss$\cdot$}\hss}\hbox{$\prec$}}}}\hat{\xi}
,
for which we have the monotonicity (
135j
).

Finally
, the two cases agree for
2\leq b
, where

\Dim_{q}
is increasing with respect to dominance,
and we do not have dominance-monotonicity for
0<b<2
.

To see (
138l
) for (
67b
),
we need to check the monotonicity of
\Dim^{\prime}_{b}

with respect to the steps (
132c
) of the dominance order.
We have to treat the
y_{j}=1
case separately,
since such a step results in
x_{j}=y_{j}-1=0
row (
132c
),
for which we have different form of the generator function.

First,
for the case
2\leq y_{j}
, we have
{\lvert\hat{\upsilon}\rvert}={\lvert\hat{\xi}\rvert}
,
so, by (
67b
), we get the same equation (
140a
) as for
\Dim_{b}
,
and have the upper relation sign for
0<b
.

Second,
for the case
y\equiv y_{j}=1
,
we again have that the step (
132c
) of the dominance order
is an arrow of the refinement (
131c
),
\hat{\upsilon}\mathrel{\mathchoice{{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to7.7778pt{\hss$\cdot$}\hss}\hbox{$\prec$}}}{{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to7.7778pt{\hss$\cdot$}\hss}\hbox{$\prec$}}}{\scriptstyle{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to6.25002pt{\hss$\cdot$}\hss}\hbox{$\prec$}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.41673pt{\hss$\cdot$}\hss}\hbox{$\prec$}}}}\hat{\xi}
,
for which we have the monotonicity (
135k
).

Finally
, the two cases agree for
0<b
, where

\Dim^{\prime}_{q}
is increasing with respect to dominance.

To see (
138m
) for (
68a
), note that
the (
138k
) dominance-monotonicity of
\Dim_{b}
is not changed
by the monotonicity of
u\mapsto\log_{b}(u)
if
2\leq b
.

To see (
138n
) for (
68b
),
note that
u\mapsto\log_{b}(u)
is increasing for
1<b
,
and for
\Dim^{\prime}_{b}
we have (
138l
).
(Note that the parameter range is constrained not to substitute negative number into the logarithm,
see at the proof of (
135m
).)

Appendix F
Bounds on the variance

In this section we write out some more or less well-known calculations
related to the variance in multipartite systems
for the convenience of the reader.

F.1
Upper bound for the variance

For any finite dimensional Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
,
for the self-adjoint operator
A\in\Lin(\mathcal{H})
given by its spectral decomposition as

A=\sum_{i=1}^{\dim(\mathcal{H})}a_{i}|\alpha_{i}\rangle\langle\alpha_{i}|
,
with
a_{\text{min}}:=\min_{i}a_{i}
and
a_{\text{max}}:=\max_{i}a_{i}
,
we have

\Var(\rho,A)\leq\frac{1}{4}(a_{\text{max}}-a_{\text{min}})^{2}.

(141)

Indeed, consider the operators

(a_{\text{max}}\mathrm{I}-A)
and
(A-a_{\text{min}}\mathrm{I})
,
which are positive semidefinite and commuting,
so
(a_{\text{max}}\mathrm{I}-A)(A-a_{\text{min}}\mathrm{I})
is also positive semidefinite,
so

\begin{split}0&\leq\Tr\bigl(\rho(a_{\text{max}}\mathrm{I}-A)(A-a_{\text{min}}\mathrm{I})\bigr)\\
&=-\Tr(\rho A^{2})+(a_{\text{max}}+a_{\text{min}})\Tr(\rho A)-a_{\text{max}}a_{\text{min}}.\end{split}

Then we have

\begin{split}&\Var(\rho,A)=\Tr(\rho A^{2})-\Tr(\rho A)^{2}\\
&\quad\leq(a_{\text{max}}+a_{\text{min}})\Tr(\rho A)-a_{\text{max}}a_{\text{min}}-\Tr(\rho A)^{2}\\
&\quad=\bigl(a_{\text{max}}-\Tr(\rho A)\bigr)\bigl(\Tr(\rho A)-a_{\text{min}}\bigr),\end{split}

(this inequality between the first and last term is called Bhatia–Davis inequality
[
117
]
)
then the inequality between the arithmetic and geometric mean
(
q=0
and
q^{\prime}=1
in (
129e
))
leads to (
141
).

The bound (
141
) can be attained by the pure state
\rho=|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|

with
|\psi\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\bigl(|\alpha_{\text{max}}\rangle+\mathrm{e}^{i\vartheta}|\alpha_{\text{min}}\rangle\bigr)
for any phase
\vartheta
,
or by the mixed state
\rho=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(|\alpha_{\text{max}}\rangle\langle\alpha_{\text{max}}|+|\alpha_{\text{min}}\rangle\langle\alpha_{\text{min}}|\bigr)
,
or by convex combinations of these,
leading to
\frac{1}{2}\bigl(|\alpha_{\text{max}}\rangle\langle\alpha_{\text{max}}|+|\alpha_{\text{min}}\rangle\langle\alpha_{\text{min}}|+c|\alpha_{\text{max}}\rangle\langle\alpha_{\text{min}}|+\bar{c}|\alpha_{\text{min}}\rangle\langle\alpha_{\text{max}}|\bigr)
for any
c\in\mathbb{C}
,
{\lvert c\rvert}\leq 1
.

The classical version of the bound (
141
)
is a standard result in classical probability theory, called Popoviciu’s inequality
[
118
]
.
Here we have seen the widely used straightforward generalization of that
for the quantum case
[
117
,
97
]
.

F.2
Upper bound for the variance of collective operators

Let us have the collective operator

A_{X}=\sum_{l\in X}A_{l}\otimes\mathrm{I}_{\overline{\{l\}}}

of subsystem
X
,
where

A_{l}=\sum_{i=1}^{\dim(\mathcal{H}_{l})}a_{i}|\alpha_{l,i}\rangle\langle\alpha_{l,i}|

have the same spectrum
for all elementary subsystems
l\in X
.
If
a_{\text{min}}:=\min_{i}\{a_{i}\}
and
a_{\text{max}}:=\max_{i}\{a_{i}\}
,
then the minimal and maximal eigenvalues of
A_{X}
are

a_{X,\text{min}}={\lvert X\rvert}a_{\text{min}}
and

a_{X,\text{max}}={\lvert X\rvert}a_{\text{max}}
,
and the bound (
141
) takes the form

\Var(\rho_{X},A_{X})\leq\frac{{\lvert X\rvert}^{2}}{4}(a_{\text{max}}-a_{\text{min}})^{2}.

(142a)

This bound can be attained by the pure state
\rho_{X}=|\psi_{X}\rangle\langle\psi_{X}|

with
|\psi_{X}\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\bigl(\bigotimes_{l\in X}|\alpha_{l,\text{max}}\rangle+\mathrm{e}^{i\vartheta}\bigotimes_{l\in X}|\alpha_{l,\text{min}}\rangle\bigr)
,
or by the mixed state
\rho_{X}=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\bigotimes_{l\in X}|\alpha_{l,\text{max}}\rangle\langle\alpha_{l,\text{max}}|+\bigotimes_{l\in X}|\alpha_{l,\text{min}}\rangle\langle\alpha_{l,\text{min}}|\bigr)
,
or by convex combinations of these,
leading to
\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\bigotimes_{l\in X}|\alpha_{l,\text{max}}\rangle\langle\alpha_{l,\text{max}}|+\bigotimes_{l\in X}|\alpha_{l,\text{min}}\rangle\langle\alpha_{l,\text{min}}|+c\bigotimes_{l\in X}|\alpha_{l,\text{max}}\rangle\langle\alpha_{l,\text{min}}|+\bar{c}\bigotimes_{l\in X}|\alpha_{l,\text{min}}\rangle\langle\alpha_{l,\text{max}}|\bigr)
for any
c\in\mathbb{C}
,
{\lvert c\rvert}\leq 1
.

In particular,
if the elementary subsystems are of spin
s\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
the collective spin operator
A_{X}:=J_{X}
arises
with
A_{l}:=J_{l}=v^{\text{x}}J^{\text{x}}_{l}+v^{\text{y}}J^{\text{y}}_{l}+v^{\text{z}}J^{\text{z}}_{l}

for the direction
v=(v^{\text{x}},v^{\text{y}},v^{\text{z}})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
,
{\lVert v\rVert}=1
.
For this we have
a_{\text{min}}=-s
and
a_{\text{max}}=s
,
and the bound (
142a
) takes the form

\Var(\rho_{X},J_{X})\leq{\lvert X\rvert}^{2}s^{2},

(142b)

which is

\Var(\rho_{X},J_{X})\leq{\lvert X\rvert}^{2}/4

(142c)

for
s=1/2
(qubits).

F.3
Variance of local operators in uncorrelated states

For
\xi
-uncorrelated state
\rho=\bigotimes_{X\in\xi}\rho_{X}

and
\xi
-local operator
A=\sum_{X\in\xi}A_{X}\otimes\mathrm{I}_{\overline{X}}

(here
\overline{X}=\{1,2,\dots,n\}\setminus X
),
we have

\Var(\rho,A)=\sum_{X\in\xi}\Var(\rho_{X},A_{X}).

(143)

(This, together with the concavity of the variance
has also been used for entanglement detection
[
119
,
120
,
121
]
.)

Indeed,

\begin{split}&\Var(\rho,A)=\Tr(\rho A^{2})-\Tr(\rho A)^{2}\\
&\quad=\Tr\Bigl(\rho\Bigl(\sum_{X\in\xi}A_{X}\otimes\mathrm{I}_{\overline{X}}\Bigr)^{2}\Bigr)-\Tr\Bigl(\rho\sum_{X\in\xi}A_{X}\otimes\mathrm{I}_{\overline{X}}\Bigr)^{2}\\
&\quad=\Tr\Bigl(\rho\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X,X^{\prime}\in\xi\\
X\neq X^{\prime}\end{subarray}}A_{X}\otimes A_{X^{\prime}}\otimes\mathrm{I}_{\overline{X\cup X^{\prime}}}\Bigr)\\
&\quad+\Tr\Bigl(\rho\sum_{X\in\xi}A_{X}^{2}\otimes\mathrm{I}_{\overline{X}}\Bigr)-\Tr\Bigl(\rho\sum_{X\in\xi}A_{X}\otimes\mathrm{I}_{\overline{X}}\Bigr)^{2}\\
&\quad=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X,X^{\prime}\in\xi\\
X\neq X^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\Tr\bigl((\rho_{X}\otimes\rho_{X^{\prime}})(A_{X}\otimes A_{X^{\prime}})\bigr)\\
&\quad+\sum_{X\in\xi}\Tr(\rho_{X}A_{X}^{2})-\Bigl(\sum_{X\in\xi}\Tr(\rho_{X}A_{X})\Bigr)^{2}\\
&\quad=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X,X^{\prime}\in\xi\\
X\neq X^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\Tr(\rho_{X}A_{X})\Tr(\rho_{X^{\prime}}A_{X^{\prime}})+\sum_{X\in\xi}\Tr(\rho_{X}A_{X}^{2})\\
&\quad-\sum_{X\in\xi}\Tr(\rho_{X}A_{X})^{2}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X,X^{\prime}\in\xi\\
X\neq X^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\Tr(\rho_{X}A_{X})\Tr(\rho_{X^{\prime}}A_{X^{\prime}})\\
&\quad=\sum_{X\in\xi}\bigl(\Tr(\rho_{X}A_{X}^{2})-\Tr(\rho_{X}A_{X})^{2}\bigr)\\
&\quad=\sum_{X\in\xi}\Var(\rho_{X},A_{X}),\end{split}

where we make use of that the state is
\xi
-uncorrelated at the
fourth equality
.

Appendix G
Metrological usefulness

In this section we
consider extremal cases of metrological usefulness,
that is, the strictness of the monotonicity (
102
) of the bounds
b_{f}(k)
.

G.1
Metrological usefulness of some one-parameter properties

Here we show that the
b_{f}(k)

bounds are
strictly
monotone (
102
) for all
k\in f(\hat{P}_{\text{I}})

in the case of producibility, partitionability and stretchability.

For producibility, given by the
w
width generator function (
23b
),
the bound
b_{\text{prod}}(k)
is given in (
81a
),
as
b_{\text{prod}}(k)=\lfloor\frac{n}{k}\rfloor k^{2}+\bigl(n-\lfloor\frac{n}{k}\rfloor k\bigr)^{2}
.
Extending this from
[1,n]_{\mathbb{N}}=w(\hat{P}_{\text{I}})

to the reals
[1,n]_{\mathbb{R}}
,
we claim that this function is continuous, and its derivative is strictly positive, although not continuous.
To see these, let us consider the intervals
(\frac{n}{m+1},\frac{n}{m}]
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
since
if
\frac{n}{m+1}<k\leq\frac{n}{m}

then
m+1>\frac{n}{k}\geq m
, so

\lfloor\frac{n}{k}\rfloor=m
.

First
, the continuity is obvious inside the intervals, it has to be checked at the boundaries of each two neighbouring intervals.
For
\frac{n}{m+1}<k\leq\frac{n}{m}
we have
\lfloor\frac{n}{k}\rfloor=m
,
and
b_{\text{prod}}(k)=mk^{2}+(n-mk)^{2}
;
for
\frac{n}{m}<k\leq\frac{n}{m-1}
we have
\lfloor\frac{n}{k}\rfloor=m-1

and
b_{\text{prod}}(k)=(m-1)k^{2}+(n-(m-1)k)^{2}
;
these agree for the value
k=\frac{n}{m}
.
(The continuity can be seen intuitively as well,
imagining the sums of squares of the rows of
the ‘Young diagrams’ of not rows of discrete boxes,
but of rows of continuously variable stripes.)

Second
, the monotonicity has to be checked for every interval.
If
k\in(\frac{n}{m+1},\frac{n}{m}]
then
\lfloor\frac{n}{k}\rfloor=m
,
and
\frac{\partial b_{\text{prod}}(k)}{\partial k}=\frac{\partial}{\partial k}(mk^{2}+(n-mk)^{2})=2m(mk+k-n)>0

which is
k>\frac{n}{m+1}
, which holds in the given interval.

For partitionability, given by the
h
height generator function (
23a
),
the bound
b_{\text{part}}(k)
is given in (
83a
),
as
b_{\text{part}}(k)=k^{2}-(2n+1)k+n(n+2)
.
Extending this from
[1,n]_{\mathbb{N}}=h(\hat{P}_{\text{I}})

to the reals
[1,n]_{\mathbb{R}}
, and differentiating by
k
we have strictly negative derivative for all
k<n+1/2
.

For stretchability, given by the
r
rank generator function (
23c
),
the bound
b_{\text{str}}(k)
is given in (
84
).
By changing to the new variable
l:=n+k\in[1,2n-1]_{\mathbb{N}}\setminus\{2,2n-2\}

the bound (
84
) takes the form

\begin{cases}n+24&\text{if $l=10$ ($n\geq 8$)},\\
n+60&\text{if $l=16$ ($n\geq 12$)},\\
(\frac{l}{2})^{2}-\frac{l}{2}+n+2&\text{if $l\in 2\mathbb{N}$},\\
(\frac{l+2}{2})^{2}-\frac{l+1}{2}+n&\text{if $l\in 2\mathbb{N}+1$},\end{cases}

(144)

the strict monotonicity of which is straightforward to check.

G.2
Minimal metrological usefulness

Here we show that
for a (nonconstant) generator function
f

there exist at least two different values of the bound
b_{f}(k)
.

Indeed, if
f
is not constant then we have

f(\bot)\neq f(\top)
by (
24
),
and
f^{\lesseqgtr}\bigl(f(\top)\bigr)=\hat{P}_{\text{I}}

while
f^{\lesseqgtr}\bigl(f(\bot)\bigr)\not\ni\top
by definition (
26b
).
Then in the two cases

b_{f}\bigl(f(\top)\bigr)=\max_{\hat{\xi}\in f^{\lesseqgtr}(f(\top))}s_{2}(\hat{\xi})=\max_{\hat{\xi}\in\hat{P}_{\text{I}}}s_{2}(\hat{\xi})=s_{2}(\top),

(145a)

and

b_{f}\bigl(f(\bot)\bigr)=\max_{\hat{\xi}\in f^{\lesseqgtr}(f(\bot))}s_{2}(\hat{\xi})<s_{2}(\top),

(145b)

where we used that
the maximum of
s_{2}
is taken on the top uniquely,

s_{2}(\hat{\xi})=n^{2}
if and only if
\hat{\xi}=\top
.
